{}^{\wedge}(\varphi^{\wedge})=(^{\wedge}\varphi)^{\wedge}
if and only if
L_{\varphi}:E\to F^{\prime}
is weakly compact. This is also equivalent to the existence of an extension of
\varphi
to
(E^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}
that is separately
w^{*}
-continuous. Note that, by the comment above, if
\varphi\in(E\otimes_{h}F)^{\prime}
then
L_{\varphi}
is weakly compact.

Note that we have the separating duality pair
\big\langle E\otimes F^{\prime\prime},(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}\big\rangle
given by

\langle z,\varphi\rangle=\varphi^{\wedge}(z).

Using the bipolar theorem it is easy to see that, in the Banach space realm, the unit ball

B_{E\otimes_{\alpha}F}
is
\sigma(E\otimes F^{\prime\prime},(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime})
-dense in the unit ball
B_{E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime}}
, whenever
\alpha
is a finitely generated tensor norm.
In other words, given
z\in B_{E\otimes\alpha F^{\prime\prime}}
there is a net
(u_{\eta})_{\eta}\subset B_{E\otimes_{\alpha}F}
such that for every
\varphi\in(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}
,

\varphi(u_{\eta})\to\varphi^{\wedge}(z).

In our setting, we can consider for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following duality pair:

\big\langle M_{n}(E\otimes F^{\prime\prime}),S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}]\big\rangle

defined as

\langle(z_{ij}),(\varphi_{ij})\rangle=\sum_{i,j}\varphi_{ij}^{\wedge}(z_{ij}).

It is easy to see that this dual pairing system is separating.
Moreover, we have the following density result.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
\alpha
be a o.s. tensor norm (on
\ONORM
),
E
and
F
be normed operator spaces.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the unit ball
B_{M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)}
is
\sigma(M_{n}(E\otimes F^{\prime\prime}),S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}])
-dense in the unit ball
B_{M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})}

in the following cases:

(a)

\alpha
is finitely-generated and
F
is locally reflexive.

(b)

\alpha
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.

Proof.

Since
\left(M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)\right)^{\prime}
is
S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}]
isometrically, it is clear that the polar
\left(B_{M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)}\right)^{\circ}
(in this pairing system) is
B_{S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}]}.

By the bipolar theorem it is enough to prove that
B_{M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})}\subset\left(B_{S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}]}\right)^{\circ}.

Indeed, given
(z_{ij})\in B_{M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})}
and
(\varphi_{ij})\in B_{S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}]}
; by the Extension Lemma
4.3
along with
[
ER00
, Thm. 4.1.8]
we know that
(\varphi_{ij}^{\wedge})\in B_{S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}]}
. Using again

\left(M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})\right)^{\prime}=S_{1}^{n}[(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}]
isometrically, we derive that that

\left|\langle(z_{ij}),(\varphi_{ij})\rangle\right|=\left|\sum_{i,j}\varphi_{ij}^{\wedge}(z_{ij})\right|\leq 1.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.
The embedding lemma

Operator space tensor norms generally do not respect subspaces, but the embedding into the bidual is respected under certain conditions.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm (on
\ONORM
).
The mapping

id_{E}\otimes\kappa_{F}:E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime}

is a complete isometry in the following cases:

(a)

\alpha
is finitely-generated and
F
is locally reflexive.

(b)

\alpha
is cofinitely-generated.

(c)

\alpha
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.

Proof.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)
.
By the complete metric mapping property

id_{E}\otimes\kappa_{F}:E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime}

is always a complete contraction, so

\alpha_{n}(z;E,F^{\prime\prime})\leq\alpha_{n}(z;E,F).

(a)
By the extension lemma,

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}(z;E,F)

\displaystyle=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle z,\Phi\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\;:\;\Phi\in M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}),\left\|\Phi\right\|_{M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime})}\leq 1\big\}

\displaystyle=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle z,\Phi^{\wedge}\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\;:\;\Phi\in M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}),\left\|\Phi\right\|_{M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime})}\leq 1\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle z,\Psi\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\;:\;\Psi\in M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}),\left\|\Psi\right\|_{M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime})}\leq 1\big\}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}(z;E,F^{\prime\prime}),

which gives the reverse inequality.

(b)
Let
L\in\OCOFIN(F)
, then
L^{00}
(formed in
F^{\prime\prime}
) is in
\OCOFIN(F^{\prime\prime})
and the map

\kappa_{F/L}:F/L\to(F/L)^{\prime\prime}=F^{\prime\prime}/L^{00}

is completely isometric and surjective; moreover,
q^{F^{\prime\prime}}_{L^{00}}\circ\kappa_{F}=\kappa_{F/L}\circ q^{F}_{L}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}\big((id_{E}\otimes q_{L}^{F})_{n}(z);E,F/L\big)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}\big((id_{E}\otimes(\kappa_{F/L}\circ q^{F}_{L}))_{n}(z);E,(F/L)^{\prime\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}\big((id_{E}\otimes q^{F^{\prime\prime}}_{L^{00}})\circ(id_{E}\otimes\kappa_{F}))_{n}(z);E,F^{\prime\prime}/L^{00}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}((id_{E}\otimes\kappa_{F}))_{n}(z);E,F^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(z;E,F^{\prime\prime})=\alpha_{n}(z;E,F^{\prime\prime})

The same argument with
E/K
instead of
E
allows us to conclude
\alpha_{n}(z;E,F)\leq\alpha_{n}(z;E,F^{\prime\prime})
.

(c)
The same proof as in the case
(a)
works here because a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm is finitely-generated and the hypothesis of local reflexivity of
F
is not needed for the extension lemma with this norm.

∎

We point out that part (c) above is a generalization of
[
AK21
, Lemma 11]
, where the spaces are assumed to be
C^{*}
-algebras and the embedding is only shown to be an isometry instead of a complete isometry.

Note that the completion of an operator space
F
has the same bidual as
F
. Therefore, as a consequence of the previous result we have:

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm (on
\ONORM
) and let
\widetilde{F}
the completion of a normed operator space
F
.
The mapping

id_{E}\otimes\iota_{F}:E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{F},

where
\iota_{F}:F\to\widetilde{F}
is the canonical inclusion,
is a complete isometry with dense range in the following cases:

(a)

\alpha
is finitely-generated and
F
is locally reflexive.

(b)

\alpha
is cofinitely-generated.

(c)

\alpha
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.

4.4.
The density lemma

We now state the so-called density lemma, which asserts that a given completely bounded mapping defined on the tensor product of certain normed operator spaces can be extended to a completely bounded mapping defined on the tensor product of the completions of the spaces. As usual, we need a local reflexivity hypothesis for the finitely-generated case. The proof easily follows from Corollary
4.8
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm (on
\ONORM
),
E,F
and
G
normed operator spaces,
E_{0}
and
F_{0}
dense subspaces of
E
and
F
respectively. Suppose
T\in CB(E\otimes_{\proj}F;G)
such that
T|_{E_{0}\otimes_{\alpha}F_{0}}\in CB(E_{0}\otimes_{\alpha}F_{0};G)
. Then
T\in CB(E\otimes_{\alpha}F;G)
and

\|T\|_{CB(E_{0}\otimes_{\alpha}F_{0};G)}=\|T\|_{CB(E\otimes_{\alpha}F;G)},

in the following cases:

(a)

\alpha
is finitely-generated and
F
is locally reflexive.

(b)

\alpha
is cofinitely-generated.

(c)

\alpha
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.

4.5.
The
\mathscr{OS}_{p}
-local technique lemma

In the Banach space setting the
\mathscr{L}_{p}
-local technique lemma allow us to transfer results from tensor products of
\ell_{p}^{n}
spaces to general
\mathscr{L}_{p}
-spaces (which, loosely speaking, are space whose finite dimensional subspaces “uniformly look” like
\ell_{p}^{n}
’s).
In the operator space setting, one of the possible extension of the
\mathscr{L}_{p}
-spaces are the
\mathscr{OS}_{p}
-spaces (see Section
2.7
for the definition).
For this extension, we have the following local technique lemma.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be o.s. tensor norms,
c\geq 0
and
E
a normed operator space such that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\alpha\leq c\beta{\quad\text{on}\quad}E\otimes S_{p}^{k}.

Then
\alpha\leq Cc\overrightarrow{\beta}{\quad\text{on}\quad}E\otimes F

for every
\mathscr{OS}_{p,C}
-space
F
.

Proof.

For
N\in\OFIN(F)
, by
[
JNRX04
, Lemma 2.1]
we can take a factorization

\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id_{N}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{S_{p}^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}

with
\left\|R\right\|_{\cb}\left\|S\right\|_{\cb}\leq C(1+\varepsilon)
. Let
M:=S(S_{p}^{k})
.
Then for every
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes N)
,

\alpha_{n}(z;E,M)=\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes(S\circ R))_{n}z;E,M)\leq\left\|S\right\|_{\cb}\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes R)_{n}z;E,S_{p}^{k})\\
\leq\left\|S\right\|_{\cb}c\beta_{n}((id_{E}\otimes R)_{n}z;E,S_{p}^{k})\leq\left\|S\right\|_{\cb}\left\|R\right\|_{\cb}c\beta_{n}(z;E,N).

The statement follows.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Note that more generally the proof above applies whenever
F
has the
\gamma_{p}
-AP
[
JNRX04
, Sec. 1]
, thanks to
[
JNRX04
, Lemma 1.4]
.
Also note that the same argument yields versions of the local technique lemma for other operator space versions of the
\mathscr{L}_{p}
spaces, e.g. the
\mathscr{OL}_{p}
spaces from
[
JNRX04
]
.

5.
Dual o.s. tensor norms

In a nutshell, the dual o.s. tensor norm
\alpha^{\prime}
for the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
is the one that makes

E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

into a complete isometry for any
E,F\in\OFIN
.
More explicitly, when
E,F\in\OFIN
for
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
we define

\alpha^{\prime}_{n}(z;E,F):=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle u,z\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\mid\alpha_{m}(u;E^{\prime},F^{\prime})\leq 1\big\}.

Let us check that, for example, the minimal and projective o.s. tensor norms are in duality with each other.
Consider finite-dimensional operator spaces
E
and
F
. The operator space structure on
E\otimes_{\min}F
is, by definition, the one induced by the embedding
E\otimes_{\min}F\to\CB(E^{\prime},F)
. Now,
(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is completely isometric to
\CB(E^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})=\CB(E^{\prime},F)
, from where we conclude that the canonical identification

E\otimes_{\min}F=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry. Taking duals and exchanging the roles of
E,F
and
E^{\prime},F^{\prime}
we also have a complete isometry

E\otimes_{\proj}F=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}.

Proposition 5.1
.

If
\alpha
is an o.s. tensor norm on
\OFIN
, then so is
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
E,F
be finite-dimensional operator spaces.
It is clear that
\alpha^{\prime}
is an operator space structure on
E\otimes F
.
From
\min\leq\alpha\leq\proj
, taking duals we conclude
\min=\proj^{\prime}\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\min^{\prime}=\proj
(for finite-dimensional spaces).
The same sort of duality argument shows that
\alpha^{\prime}
is uniform.
∎

The finite hull
\overrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}
of
\alpha^{\prime}
will be called the
dual o.s. tensor norm

\alpha^{\prime}
(on
\ONORM
) of the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
(on
\OFIN
or
\ONORM
).

The following properties are easy to check:

Proposition 5.2
.

(a)

If
\alpha\leq c\beta
, then
\beta^{\prime}\leq c\alpha^{\prime}
.

(b)

\alpha=\alpha^{\prime\prime}
on
\OFIN
, and
\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\alpha^{\prime\prime}
.

(c)

\alpha=\alpha^{\prime\prime}
on
\ONORM
if and only if
\alpha
is finitely-generated.

Since
\min
and
\proj
are finitely-generated,
\min^{\prime}=\proj
and
\proj^{\prime}=\min
. Also,
h^{\prime}=h
(see, for instance,
[
ER00
, Chapter 9]
).
Additionally, the symmetrized Haagerup o.s. tensor norms are in duality with each other.

Proposition 5.3
.

(h\cap h^{t})^{\prime}=h+h^{t}
and
(h+h^{t})^{\prime}=h\cap h^{t}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.6
both
h\cap h^{t}
and
h+h^{t}
are finitely-generated, so it suffices to check that they are in duality for finite-dimensional spaces. Let
E,F\in\OFIN
. By taking the adjoint of the canonical complete isometry
E\otimes_{h\cap h^{t}}F\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{h}F)\oplus_{\infty}(E\otimes_{h^{t}}F)
we get a canonical complete quotient

(E\otimes_{h}F)^{\prime}\oplus_{1}(E\otimes_{h^{t}}F)^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow(E\otimes_{h\cap h^{t}}F)^{\prime}
.
But the self-duality of
h
and
h^{t}
implies
(E\otimes_{h}F)^{\prime}=E^{\prime}\otimes_{h}F^{\prime}
and
(E\otimes_{h^{t}}F)^{\prime}=E^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{t}}F^{\prime}
, so we get a complete quotient

(E^{\prime}\otimes_{h}F^{\prime})\oplus_{1}(E^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{t}}F^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow(E\otimes_{h\cap h^{t}}F)^{\prime}
. This is exactly the same quotient giving the o.s. structure to
E^{\prime}\otimes_{h+h^{t}}F^{\prime}
, and thus
E^{\prime}\otimes_{h+h^{t}}F^{\prime}=(E\otimes_{h\cap h^{t}}F)^{\prime}
as claimed.
∎

Definition 5.4
.

Given an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
, its
adjoint or contragradient
o.s. tensor norm is defined as
\alpha^{*}=(\alpha^{t})^{\prime}=(\alpha^{\prime})^{t}
.

As in the classical case, the following theorem tells us in what sense the completely isometric embedding

E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

valid for finite-dimensional spaces, extends to infinite-dimensional ones.

Theorem 5.5

(The duality theorem)
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm (on
\OFIN
) and let
E
,
F
be normed operator spaces. The following natural mappings are complete isometries:

(5.1)

\displaystyle E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

(5.2)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\qquad\textrm{ whenever }E\textrm{ is locally reflexive}

(5.3)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime}\qquad\textrm{ whenever }E\textrm{ and }F\textrm{ are locally reflexive}

Proof.

To prove (
5.1
), observe first that

\OFIN(E^{\prime})=\{K^{0}\mid K\in\OCOFIN(E)\}

and that for
(K,L)\in\OCOFIN(E)\times\OCOFIN(F)
,
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
and
u\in M_{m}(K^{0}\otimes L^{0})\subset M_{m}(E^{\prime}\otimes F^{\prime})
,
we have

{\langle\langle z,u\rangle\rangle}={\langle\langle(q_{K}^{E}\otimes q_{L}^{F})(z),u\rangle\rangle}

Now, by the valid duality relation for finite-dimensional spaces,

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(z;E,F)

\displaystyle=\sup_{K,L}\left\{\alpha_{n}\big((q_{K}^{E}\otimes q_{L}^{F})(z);E/K,F/L\big)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{K,L}\sup\left\{\left\|{\langle\langle(q_{K}^{E}\otimes q_{L}^{F})(z),u\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}_{m}(u;K^{0},L^{0})<1\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\left\|{\langle\langle z,u\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\mid\overrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}_{m}(u;E^{\prime},F^{\prime})<1\right\}

and this is precisely (
5.1
).

Consider now the commutative diagram

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{1}}
\textstyle{(E^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{2}}

Note that
\phi_{1}
is a complete isometry by (
5.1
), whereas
\phi_{2}
is a complete isometry by
the Extension Lemma
4.3
; (
5.2
) follows.
In the same way, in the diagram

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\textstyle{(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}}

\psi_{1}
is a complete isometry by (
5.2
) and
\psi_{2}
is a complete isometry the Extension Lemma
4.3
; (
5.3
) follows.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

The Extension Lemma
4.3
is valid without the hypothesis of local reflexivity for
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norms.
Hence, in equations (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) of the Duality Theorem, if
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
\lambda
-tensor norm the local reflexivity of the involved spaces is not needed.

In particular, for
\alpha^{\prime}=\proj
, recalling that
\overleftarrow{\min}=\min
we obtain for any normed operator spaces
E
and
F
the following known completely isometric embeddings

E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime}.

One nice consequence of the duality theorem is the following:

Corollary 5.7
.

If
E
and
F
are normed operator spaces with the completely metric approximation property,
then the natural mappings

E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

are complete isometries.

Proof.

Proposition
3.2
and Corollary
4.2
imply the complete isometry between
E\otimes_{\alpha}F
and
E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F
, now (
5.1
) implies the rest.
∎

Observe that if we replace the CMAP property by the CBAP property the above identifications become complete isomorphisms (with norms controlled by the CBAP constant).

We now show that the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm is both finitely and cofinitely-generated.

Remark 5.8
.

Let
h
be the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm. Then,

h=\overleftarrow{h}=\overrightarrow{h}.

Proof.

The fact that
h=\overrightarrow{h}
easily follows since the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm respects complete isometries
[
ER00
, Proposition 9.25]
.

To see that
h=\overleftarrow{h}
notice that by the Duality Theorem
5.5
the canonical embedding below
is a complete isometry

(5.4)

E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{h}}F\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{h^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{h}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

where we have used in the last equality the self-dual property of the o.s. tensor norm
h

[
ER00
, Corollary 9.4.8]
.

On the other hand, by
[
ER00
, Theorem 9.4.7.]
and the Embedding Lemma
4.7
we have that

(5.5)

E\otimes_{h}F\hookrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{h}F^{\prime\prime}\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{h}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

is a complete isometry.

Since both inclusions in equations (
5.4
), (
5.5
) are complete isometries,
h
and
\overleftarrow{h}
must coincide as o.s. tensor norms.
∎

In general, o.s. tensor norms are not both finitely and cofinitely-generated, as we have just seen for the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm and the minimal o.s. tensor norm.
For many purposes it is often enough that the equality
\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\alpha}

holds just for finite dimensional spaces or locally reflexive spaces. This motivates the following definitions that we will use in the sequel.

Definition 5.9
.

An o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
is called
right-accessible
if

\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;M,F)=\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;M,F)\qquad\text{for all }M\in\OFIN,F\in\ONORM,n\in{\mathbb{N}}

and
left-accessible
if

\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;E,M)=\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;E,M)\qquad\text{for all }M\in\OFIN,E\in\ONORM,n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

An o.s. tensor norm that is both left and right-accessible is called
accessible
, and
totally accessible
means that
\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\alpha}
.

In many cases, local reflexivity naturally appears when dealing with accessibility. Based on this we introduce the following:

Definition 5.10
.

An o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
is called
locally right-accessible if

\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;M,F)=\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;M,F)\qquad\text{for all }M\in\OFIN,F\in\OLOC,n\in{\mathbb{N}}

and
locally left-accessible
if

\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;E,M)=\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot;E,M)\qquad\text{for all }M\in\OFIN,E\in\OLOC,n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

An o.s. tensor norm that is both locally left and locally right-accessible is called
locally accessible
, and
locally totally accessible
means that
\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\alpha}
in
\OLOC\otimes\OLOC
.

We have already seen that the minimal and the Haagerup o.s. tensor norms are both finitely and cofinitely-generated, or in other words they are totally accessible.
Recall that, as we saw in Proposition
3.3
, the projective o.s. tensor norm is not cofinitely-generated, hence it is not totally accessible. Nevertheless, as in the Banach space framework,
\proj
is indeed accessible.
To see this, let
E\in\ONORM
and
M\in\OFIN
. We always have that the natural mapping

(5.6)

E\otimes_{\proj}M\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}M^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry (see
[
ER90
, Theorem 2.2]
or Remark
8.11
). On the other hand, as a consequence of the Duality Theorem
5.5
we also have that the mapping

E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\proj}}M\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}M^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry. Thus, both o.s. tensor norms,
\overrightarrow{\proj}=\proj
and
\overleftarrow{\proj}
, must coincide on
E\otimes M
. This shows the left-accessibility of
\proj
. Analogously, we have that
\proj
is also right-accessible and therefore accessible.

More generally, a similar argument will show that any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm is accessible.
Let us start with a formula for calculating the dual of a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm, which is essentially
[
Wie09
, Prop. 5.3]
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
E
and
F
be operator spaces, and
u\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
. For any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm,

\lambda^{\prime}_{n}(u;E,F)=\sup\Big\{\left\|\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)\big)_{n}(u)\right\|_{M_{n\tau(k)}}\;:\;k\in{\mathbb{N}},\left\|\phi\right\|_{M_{k}(E^{\prime})},\left\|\psi\right\|_{M_{k}(F^{\prime})}\leq 1\Big\}

Proof.

Assume first that
E
and
F
are finite-dimensional.
By the definition of
\lambda^{\prime}
and Theorem
2.4
,

E\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}F^{\prime})^{\prime}=\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime}\times F^{\prime}).

Therefore,
\lambda^{\prime}_{n}(u;E,F)
is the norm
\left\|\Phi_{u}\right\|_{\cb,\lambda}
of the bilinear map
\Phi_{u}:E^{\prime}\times F^{\prime}\to M_{n}
associated to
u
.
By definition, said norm is

\left\|\Phi_{u}\right\|_{\cb,\lambda}=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big\{\left\|(\Phi_{u})_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)\right\|_{M_{n\tau(k)}}\;:\;\left\|\phi\right\|_{M_{k}(E^{\prime})},\left\|\psi\right\|_{M_{k}(F^{\prime})}\leq 1\},

so it suffices to check

\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)\big)_{n}(u)=(\Phi_{u})_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)

which follows easily from the definitions.

Let us now consider the case of arbitrary
E
and
F
.
Let
E_{0}\in\OFIN(E)
and
F_{0}\in\OFIN(F)
such that

u\in M_{n}(E_{0}\otimes F_{0})
.
By the previous argument,

\lambda^{\prime}_{n}(u;E_{0},F_{0})=\sup\Big\{\left\|\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\big)_{n}(u)\right\|_{M_{n\tau(k)}}\;:\;k\in{\mathbb{N}},\left\|\phi_{0}\right\|_{M_{k}(E_{0}^{\prime})},\left\|\psi_{0}\right\|_{M_{k}(F_{0}^{\prime})}\leq 1\Big\}.

By the Arveson extension theorem,
\phi_{0}\in M_{k}(E_{0}^{\prime})=\CB(E_{0},M_{k})
and
\psi_{0}\in M_{k}(F_{0}^{\prime})=\CB(F_{0},M_{k})

admit respective norm-preserving extensions
\phi\in\CB(E,M_{k})
and
\psi\in\CB(F,M_{k})
.
Since clearly

\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)\big)_{n}(u)=\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\big)_{n}(u),

it follows that

\lambda^{\prime}_{n}(u;E_{0},F_{0})=\sup\Big\{\left\|\big(\otimes_{B^{\lambda}_{k}}(\phi,\psi)\big)_{n}(u)\right\|_{M_{n\tau(k)}}\;:\;k\in{\mathbb{N}},\left\|\phi\right\|_{M_{k}(E^{\prime})},\left\|\psi\right\|_{M_{k}(F^{\prime})}\leq 1\Big\},

and therefore
\lambda^{\prime}_{n}(u;E,F)
is also equal to the same quantity.
∎

As an immediate consequence, we have the following result (which in
[
Wie09
, Def. 5.1]
is taken as the definition for the dual of
\lambda
).

Corollary 5.12
.

Let
E
and
F
be operator spaces. For any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm, the natural map

E\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F\hookrightarrow\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime}\times F^{\prime})

is a complete isometry.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
E
and
F
be operator spaces. For any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm, the natural map

E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\CB_{\lambda}(E\times F)

is a complete isometry.

Proof.

From the previous result, the natural map

(5.7)

E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime\prime}\times F^{\prime\prime})

is a complete isometry.
As we have already observed (see the proof of the Extension Lemma
4.3
), we also have a canonical completely isometric embedding

(5.8)

\CB_{\lambda}(E\times F)\hookrightarrow\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime\prime}\times F^{\prime\prime}).

It is clear that the image of the map in (
5.7
) is contained in that of the map in (
5.8
), which gives the desired result.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
E
and
F
be operator spaces, with
E
finite-dimensional. For any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm, the natural map

E\otimes_{\lambda}F\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry.

Proof.

From the previous result, the natural map

E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime\prime}\times F^{\prime\prime})

is a complete isometry, and since
E
is finite-dimensional it follows that it is surjective and therefore

E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime}=\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime\prime}\times F^{\prime\prime})
.
Taking duals and using Theorem
2.4
we have

(E\otimes_{\lambda}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime\prime}=\CB_{\lambda}(E^{\prime\prime}\times F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

which, together with the Embedding Lemma
4.7
, the desired result follows.
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

Any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm is accessible.

Proof.

Let
E
and
F
be operator spaces, with
E
finite-dimensional. From the previous result the natural map

E\otimes_{\lambda}F\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry, and from the Duality Theorem
5.5
so is

E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\lambda}}F\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

which means that
E\otimes_{\lambda}F=E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\lambda}}F
and therefore
\lambda
is right-accessible.
The proof for left-accessibility is analogous.
∎

Left-accessibility of an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
provides a complete isometry analogous to the one that appears (for the o.s. tensor norm
\proj
) in Equation (
5.6
). This fact combined with a CMAP hypothesis imply the following result.

Corollary 5.16
.

(a)

Let
E\in\ONORM
,
F
a normed operator space with the CMAP and
\alpha
a left-accessible o.s. tensor norm. Then,

E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

are complete isometries.

(b)

Let
E\in\OLOC
,
F
a normed operator space with the CMAP and
\alpha
a locally left-accessible o.s. tensor norm. Then,

E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F\qquad\text{and}\qquad E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

are complete isometries.

(c)

The analogous results hold with right-accessibility/local right-accesibility.

Proof.

We will only prove
(a)
since the proofs of the other statements are similar. Note that, by the left-accesibility of the o.s. tensor norm we have

(5.9)

\overrightarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\alpha}\qquad\text{on}\qquad E\otimes M

for every
M\in\OFIN(F).
Now by the Approximation Lemma
4.1
, since
F
has the CMAP then
\overrightarrow{\alpha}
and
\overleftarrow{\alpha}
coincide on
E\otimes F
, and therefore

E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F

is a complete isometry.
The fact that
E\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is also a complete isometry follows from the previous identification and the Duality Theorem
5.5
.
∎

Note that by Proposition
3.2
in the statement of the previous corollary we can change
\overrightarrow{\alpha}
by
\alpha
and, of course, everything remains the same.
Let us now consider the issue of the accessibility of dual o.s. tensor norms.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm on
\ONORM
.

(a)

\alpha
is right-accessible (resp. left-accessible, accessible) then
\alpha^{\prime}
is locally right-accessible (resp. locally left-accessible, locally accessible).

(b)

If
\alpha
is accessible then the transposed o.s. tensor norm
\alpha^{t}
is accessible and the adjoint o.s. tensor norm
\alpha^{*}
is locally accessible.

Proof.

Assume that
\alpha
is right-accessible. Then for all
M\in\OFIN
and
F\in\OLOC
we have complete isometries

M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}F^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\overrightarrow{\alpha}}F^{\prime}=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F^{\prime}=(M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime}

where the first equality follows from Proposition
5.2
,
the second one from right-accessibility, and the third one from the Duality Theorem
5.5
.
Therefore we also have complete isometries

M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F\hookrightarrow(M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime\prime}=(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime},

which means that
\overleftarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}=\alpha^{\prime}=\overrightarrow{\alpha^{\prime}}
(again by the Duality Theorem) in
\OFIN\otimes\OLOC
.
This shows that
\alpha^{\prime}
is locally right-accessible.
Part (b) follows trivially.
∎

In the Banach space setting the dual of a right(left)-accessible tensor norm is again right(left)-accessible. For operator spaces we obtain in the previous proposition a weaker statement since we have proved that the dual norm is
locally
right(left)-accessible. However, we do not know whether this notion is truly weaker since we do not have any example of an o.s. tensor norm which is locally right(left)-accessible but not right(left)-accessible.

6.
The completely bounded approximation property

Recall that given
C\geq 1
, a normed operator space is said to have the
C
-completely bounded approximation property
(
C
-CBAP for short) if there exists a net of finite-rank mappings
\phi_{i}:E\to E
such that
\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\left\|\phi_{i}(x)-x\right\|\to 0
.
One of the goals in this section is to study the CBAP via various conditions involving tensor products, in the spirit of the characterizations for the operator space approximation property appearing in
[
ER00
, Sec. 11.2]
. We also deal with a weaker version, called W*CBAP, which carries over many of the interesting equivalences that appear in the classical theory of tensor products
[
DF93
, Sect. 16]
.

For a normed operator space
E
, we use the notation
K_{\infty}(E)=\mathcal{K}\otimes_{\min}E
to emphasize that we think of this space as consisting of infinite
E
-valued matrices; see
[
ER00
, Sec. 10.1]
for the specific details of how to interpret
K_{\infty}(E)
as a completion of the union of the spaces
M_{n}(E)
of
E
-valued matrices.
Given normed operator spaces
E
and
F
,
recall that a net
(\varphi^{\alpha})
in
\CB(E,F)
converges to
\varphi
in the
stable point-norm topology
if for every
x\in K_{\infty}(E)
we have that

\varphi^{\alpha}_{\infty}(x)\to\varphi_{\infty}(x)

in the norm topology of
K_{\infty}(F)

[
ER00
, Sec. 11.2]
.

Our first characterization of CBAP (Proposition
6.2
below) corresponds to
[
ER00
, Lemma 11.2.1]
, but instead of the stable point-norm topology we simply use the point-norm topology (which we denote by
\tau
).
The reason is clarified in the following remark.

Remark 6.1
.

Observe that for a bounded net in
\CB(E,F)
, convergence in the stable point-norm topology coincides with convergence in the point-norm topology. Indeed, suppose
\varphi^{\alpha},\varphi\in\CB(E,F)
and
x\in K_{\infty}(E)
. If
P^{m}:K_{\infty}(E)\to K_{\infty}(E)
denotes the truncation operator,

\left\|\varphi^{\alpha}_{\infty}(x)-\varphi_{\infty}(x)\right\|_{K_{\infty}(F)}\leq\left\|\varphi^{\alpha}\right\|_{\cb}\left\|x-P^{m}(x)\right\|_{K_{\infty}(E)}\\
+\left\|\varphi^{\alpha}_{m}(P^{m}(x))-\varphi_{m}(P^{m}(x))\right\|_{M_{m}(F)}+\left\|\varphi\right\|_{\cb}\left\|P^{m}(x)-x\right\|_{K_{\infty}(E)},

which shows that when
\varphi^{\alpha}
is bounded, convergence in the point-norm topology implies convergence in the stable point-norm topology (the other implication is trivial and holds in general).

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose that
E
is a normed operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

E
has the
C
-CBAP.

(b)

C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
is
\tau
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,E)}
.

(c)

For any normed operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F}
is
\tau
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,F)}
.

(d)

For any normed operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{F^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
is
\tau
-dense in
B_{\CB(F,E)}
.

Proof.

(a)\Rightarrow(b)
:
Suppose that
E
has
C
-CBAP, so that there exists a net of finite-rank mappings
\phi_{i}:E\to E
such that
\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\left\|x-\phi_{i}x\right\|\to 0
. This precisely means that
\phi_{i}\xrightarrow{\tau}id_{E}
, and
note that each
\phi_{i}
can be identified with an element of
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
.
For any other
\varphi\in B_{\CB(E,E)}
, the net
(\phi_{i}\circ\varphi)_{i}
does the job.
Reversing the argument gives
(b)\Rightarrow(a)
.

Now, if
(\phi_{i})
is a net in
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
converging to
id_{E}
in the
\tau
topology, then for any
\psi\in B_{\CB(E,F)}
the net
\psi\circ\phi_{i}\in C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F}
converges to
\psi
in the
\tau
topology.
Similarly, for
\psi\in B_{\CB(F,E)}
the net
\phi_{i}\circ\psi\in C\cdot B_{F^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
converges to
\psi
in the
\tau
topology. This proves
(b)\Rightarrow(c)
and
(b)\Rightarrow(d)
.
The implications
(c)\Rightarrow(b)
and
(d)\Rightarrow(b)
are clear by specializing to
F=E
.
∎

It should be noted that although completeness plays an important role in
[
ER00
, Sec. 11.2]
, we do not assume it.
This was to be expected, as the same is true of the Banach space case:
note that in
[
DF93
]
the section on BAP deals with normed spaces, whereas the one on AP deals with Banach spaces.
In this regard we point out the following easy lemma, which shows that when it comes to the CBAP it makes no difference to work with a space or with its completion.
As usual,
\widetilde{E}
denotes the completion of
E
.
Moreover, given
\varphi\in\CB(E,F)
we denote by
\widetilde{\varphi}
its unique extension to a map in
\CB(\widetilde{E},\widetilde{F})
.

Lemma 6.3
.

E
has
C
-CBAP if and only if
\widetilde{E}
has
C
-CBAP.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Let
(\varphi^{\alpha})
be a net of finite-rank maps in
\CB(E,E)
of
\cb
-norm at most
C
converging to the identity of
E
in the point-norm topology.
Let
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{E}
. By a triangle inequality argument, for
x\in E

\left\|\widetilde{\varphi}^{\alpha}(\widetilde{x})-\widetilde{x}\right\|\leq\left\|\widetilde{\varphi}^{\alpha}\right\|\left\|\widetilde{x}-x\right\|+\left\|\varphi^{\alpha}(x)-x\right\|+\left\|x-\widetilde{x}\right\|,

which shows
(\widetilde{\varphi}^{\alpha})
is a net of finite-rank maps of
\cb
-norm at most
C
converging to the identity of
\widetilde{E}
in the point-norm topology.

(\Leftarrow)
Let
(\psi^{\alpha})
be a net of finite-rank maps in
\CB(\widetilde{E},\widetilde{E})
of
\cb
-norm at most
C
converging to the identity of
\widetilde{E}
in the point-norm topology.
For each
\alpha
let
E_{\alpha}
be the range of
\psi^{\alpha}
, and for each
\delta>0

use perturbation to find a linear map
R_{\delta}:E_{\alpha}\to E
with

\left\|R_{\delta}-Id_{E_{\alpha}}\right\|_{\cb}<\delta
.
Let
\varphi^{\alpha,\delta}
be the restriction to
E
of
\frac{1}{1+\delta}R_{\delta}\circ\psi^{\alpha}
.
Then for any
x\in E
,

\left\|\varphi^{\alpha,\delta}(x)-x\right\|\leq\frac{1}{1+\delta}\left\|R_{\delta}\psi^{\alpha}(x)-\psi^{\alpha}(x)\right\|+\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\left\|\psi^{\alpha}(x)\right\|+\left\|\psi^{\alpha}(x)-x\right\|,

so
(\varphi^{\alpha,\delta})
is a net of finite-rank maps in
\CB(E,E)
of
\cb
-norm at most
C
converging to the identity of
E
in the point-norm topology.
∎

For normed operator spaces
E
and
F
,
recall that a net
(\varphi^{\alpha})
in
\CB(E,F)
converges to
\varphi
in the
stable point-weak topology
(denoted
\tau_{w}
) if for every
x\in K_{\infty}(E)
we have that

\varphi^{\alpha}_{\infty}(x)\to\varphi_{\infty}(x)

in the weak topology of
K_{\infty}(F)

[
ER00
, Sec. 11.2]
.

Let us remark that although
[
ER00
, Sec. 11.2]
works with Banach operator spaces, the proof of
[
ER00
, Prop. 11.2.2]
applies more generally to normed operator spaces.
Therefore, the proof of
[
ER00
, Cor. 11.2.3]
together with Proposition
6.2
yield the following result.

Corollary 6.4
.

Suppose that
E
is a normed operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

E
has the
C
-CBAP.

(b)

C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
is
\tau_{w}
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,E)}
.

(c)

For any operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F}
is
\tau_{w}
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,F)}
.

(d)

For any operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{F^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
is
\tau_{w}
-dense in
B_{\CB(F,E)}
.

Consider now the natural embedding

\CB(E,F)\subseteq\CB(E,F^{\prime\prime})=(E\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime})^{\prime}.

In
[
ER00
, Lemma 11.2.4]
it is proved that when
E
and
F
are complete,
the stable point-weak topology on
\CB(E,F)
is just the relative weak
∗
topology determined by
E\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime}
, and thus each stable point-weakly continuous functional on
\CB(E,F)
is determined by an element of
E\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F^{\prime}
.
Let us once again remark that the proof of
[
ER00
, Lemma 11.2.4]
applies more generally to normed operator spaces.

The bounded approximation property, in the context of normed spaces, has many important equivalences in the tensor product realm. Indeed, a Banach space
E
has the
C
-approximation property if and only if
E\otimes_{\pi}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\varepsilon}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is a
C
-isomorphism onto its image. Moreover, these properties are also equivalent to the following inequality:

\pi\leq C\overleftarrow{\pi}
on
E\otimes F
(for all normed spaces
F
).
At first glance the CBAP seems to be a nice extension of the bounded approximation property to operator spaces, and the literature shows a number of examples where the analogy works very well.
However, the lack of local reflexivity does not directly allow the equivalence between CBAP and the natural operator space versions of the mentioned results related to tensor products. Nevertheless, we will see that a weak version of CBAP introduced in
[
ER90
]
will allow us to translate those approximation properties relative to tensor products of normed spaces into this context.

The following is the obvious adaptation of the notion of W*MAP defined in
[
ER90
]
.

Definition 6.5
.

An operator space
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP if there exists a net of finite-rank maps
\phi_{i}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\phi_{i}(x)\to\kappa_{E}(x)
in the weak* topology.

Note that clearly the
C
-CBAP implies the
C
-W*CBAP, as convergence in norm implies convergence in the weak* topology.
The same argument as in
[
ER90
]
shows that if
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP, then so does
M_{n}(E)
.

Remark 6.6
.

Similarly as in
[
ER90
]
it is easy to prove (taking adjoints) that
E
has the
C
-CBAP if and only if there is a net of weak* continuous finite-rank maps
\psi_{i}:E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
such that
\|\psi_{i}\|_{cb}\leq C
for all
i
and
\psi_{i}(x^{\prime})\to x^{\prime}
in the weak* topology for every
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. Instead,
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP if and only if the same condition as above holds with the exception that the weak* continuity of the mappings
\psi_{i}
is not assumed/required.

We now prove a variant of Corollary
6.4
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Suppose that
E
is a normed operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

E
has the
C
-W*CBAP.

(b)

C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime}}
is point-weak*-dense in
B_{\CB(E,E^{\prime\prime})}
.

(c)

For any operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}}
is point-weak*-dense in
B_{\CB(E,F^{\prime})}
.

Proof.

The implication
(a)\Rightarrow(c)
is implicit in the proof of
[
ER90
, Thm. 2.2]
. Suppose that
T:E\to F^{\prime}
is a complete contraction. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{E^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{F}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{E}}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}}

Note that both
T^{\prime\prime}
and
\kappa_{F}^{\prime}
are weak* continuous.
Therefore, if
\phi_{i}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
is the net of maps in
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E}
converging to
\kappa_{E}
in the point-weak* topology given by the definition of
C
-W*CBAP, it is clear that
\kappa_{F}^{\prime}T^{\prime\prime}\phi_{i}:E\to F^{\prime}
converges to
T
in the point-weak* topology.

(c)\Rightarrow(b)
is trivial.

Finally,
(b)
implies that the canonical inclusion
\kappa_{E}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
can be approximated in the point-weak* topology by a net of finite-rank maps whose cb-norms are at most
C
, which is exactly the definition of
C
-W*CBAP, yielding
(a)
.

∎

The following result is, in a sense, a quantitative version of
[
ER90
, Thm. 2.2]
.

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
E
be an operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

For any operator space
F
, the natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is a complete
C
-isomorphism onto its image.

(b)

For any operator space
F
, the natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is a
C
-isomorphism onto its image.

(c)

E
has the
C
-W*CBAP.

(d)

For any complete contraction
T:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
there exists a net of finite-rank maps
\phi_{i}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\phi_{i}(x)\to Tx
in the weak* topology.

(e)

For any operator space
F
and any complete contraction
T:E\to F^{\prime}
, there is a net of finite-rank maps
\psi_{i}:E\to F^{\prime}
such that
\left\|\psi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\psi_{i}x\to Tx
in the weak* topology.

(f)

The natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}E^{\prime}\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}
is a complete
C
-isomorphism onto its image.

(g)

The natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}E^{\prime}\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}
is a
C
-isomorphism onto its image.

Proof.

(a)\Rightarrow(b)
is obvious. Let us assume
(b)
, and let

F
be an operator space. In order to show that the natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
is a complete
C
-isomorphism onto its image, it suffices to show that its adjoint
(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime\prime}\to(E\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime}=\CB(E,F^{\prime})
is a complete
C
-quotient. In turn, this will follow from proving that for any
n
the amplification
M_{n}((E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime\prime})\to M_{n}(\CB(E,F^{\prime}))
is a
C
-quotient. Considering the standard identifications

M_{n}((E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime\prime})=(M_{n}(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}))^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}M_{n}(F^{\prime}))^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}S_{1}^{n}[F]^{\prime})^{\prime\prime}
and

\CB(E,M_{n}(F^{\prime}))=\CB(E,S_{1}^{n}[F]^{\prime})
, we need to show that the natural map

(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}(S_{1}^{n}[F])^{\prime})^{\prime\prime}\to\CB(E,S_{1}^{n}[F]^{\prime})
is a
C
-quotient. This follows from
(b)
with
S_{1}^{n}[F]
in place of
F
and taking adjoints. Thus,
(a)\Leftrightarrow(b)
.

Now,
(c)\Leftrightarrow(d)\Leftrightarrow(e)
is just a restatement of Proposition
6.7
.

Let us now prove that
(b)\Leftrightarrow(e)
.
First observe that the natural map
E\otimes_{\proj}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
always has cb-norm one.
Therefore,
(b)
is equivalent to having

(E\otimes F)\cap B_{(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}}\subset C\cdot B_{E\otimes_{\proj}F}

By the bipolar theorem, this in turn is equivalent to

B_{\CB(E,F^{\prime})}\subset\overline{C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}}}^{w^{*}}.

where the closure is in the weak* topology associated with the identification
\CB(E,F^{\prime})=(E\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime}
. And this is clearly equivalent to the statement of
(e)
.

Finally, it is evident that
(a)\Rightarrow(f)\Rightarrow(g)
and arguing as in
(b)\Leftrightarrow(e)
we easily obtain that
(g)\Leftrightarrow(d)
.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

In view of our preceding results, it seems plausible to extend the usual concept of the bounded approximation property with respect to a tensor norm (see e.g.
[
DF93
, 21.7]
) to our operator space setting. Namely, a natural definition to consider should be the following: an operator space
E
has the
\alpha
-W*CBAP (with constant
C>0
) if the natural map

F\otimes_{\alpha}E\to(F^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}E^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete
C
-isomorphism onto its image for every operator space
F
.
Nevertheless, we do not tackle this here and leave it for future research.

The following two results follow similarly as
[
ER90
, Prop. 2.3]
and
[
ER90
, Prop. 2.4]
. We include somewhat alternative proofs for completeness.

Proposition 6.10
.

If an operator space
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP and is locally reflexive, then
E
has the
C
-CBAP.

Proof.

If
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP there is a net of finite-rank maps
\phi_{i}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\|\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
for all
i
and for every
x\in E
,
\phi_{i}(x)\to\kappa_{E}(x)
in the weak* topology. For each
i
, let us denote the range of the mapping
\phi_{i}
by
F_{i}\in\OFIN(E^{\prime\prime})
. Since
E
is locally reflexive for each
i
, there is a net of complete contractions
(\psi_{\eta}^{i})_{\eta}\subset\CB(F_{i},E)
approximating the inclusion
F_{i}\hookrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}
in the point-weak* topology. By composing we obtain a net of finite rank mappings
(\psi_{\eta}^{i}\circ\phi_{i})\subset\CB(E,E)
which converges in the point-weak topology to the identity
id:E\to E
satisfying
\left\|\psi_{\eta}^{i}\circ\phi_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq C
. A classical convex combinations argument allow us to obtain a net with the same properties but which converges in the point-norm topology to the identity; thus concluding that
E
has the C-CBAP.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

If
E
is an operator space such that
E^{\prime}
has the
C
-W*CBAP, then so does
E
.

Proof.

If
E^{\prime}
has the
C
-W*CBAP, by Theorem
6.8

(a)
(applied to
F=E
) we obtain that the map
E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}E\to(E^{\prime\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime})^{\prime}
is a complete
C
-isomorphism with its image. Since both
\proj
and
\min
are symmetric we can flip the spaces (by transposition) to obtain that
E\otimes_{\proj}E^{\prime}\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}
is a complete
C
-isomorphism with its image. This is exactly what is stated in Theorem
6.8

(f)
. Hence,
E
has the
C
-W*CBAP.
∎

As a corollary we see that under local reflexivity the
C
-CBAP can be transferred from the dual
E^{\prime}
to
E
.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
E
be an operator space which is locally reflexive. If
E^{\prime}
has the
C
-CBAP, then so does
E
.
(in fact, if
E^{\prime}
has the
C
-W*CBAP then
E
has
C
-CBAP).

As a consequence of Theorem
6.8
we obtain the following analogue of
[
DF93
, Thm. 16.2]
:

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
E
be an operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

E
has the
C
-W*CBAP.

(b)

For every operator space
F
(or only
F=E^{\prime}
), the identity
E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\proj}}F\to E\otimes_{\proj}F
has
\cb
-norm at most
C
.

(c)

For every operator space
F
, the identity
E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\proj}}F\to E\otimes_{\proj}F
has norm at most
C
.

In particular,
E
has the W*CMAP if and only if

\proj=\overleftarrow{\proj}\quad\text{ on }E\otimes F

for every operator space
F
(or only
F=E^{\prime}
).

Proof.

By the Duality Theorem
5.5
we have the complete isometry
E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\proj}}F\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
. Also, we know that
\overleftarrow{\proj}\leq\proj
. Therefore,

E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\proj}}F\to E\otimes_{\proj}F
has
\cb
-norm at most
C
(resp. norm at most
C
)
if and only if the mapping

E\otimes_{\proj}F\to(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete
C
-isomorphism (resp.
C
-isomorphism) onto its image. Now, Theorem
6.8
gives the result.
∎

Now we present an operator space version of
[
DF93
, Lem. 16.2]
which enables us to prove the subsequent corollary.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm,
E
and
F
be normed operator spaces, and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
\alpha_{n}\leq C\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}
on
E\otimes F
if and only if

B_{M_{n}\big((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}\big)}\subset C\cdot\overline{B_{M_{n}(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})}}^{\tau_{w}}\subset M_{n}\big(\CB(E,F^{\prime})\big)=\CB\big(E,M_{n}(F^{\prime})\big).

Proof.

Let
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
. By the Duality Theorem
5.5
,

\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}(z;E,F)=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle T,z\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\mid T\in B_{M_{m}(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})}\big\},

whereas clearly

\alpha_{n}(z;E,F)=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle T,z\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\mid T\in B_{M_{m}((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime})}\big\}

By the bipolar theorem, the inclusion

B_{M_{n}\big((E\otimes_{\alpha}F)^{\prime}\big)}\subset C\cdot\overline{B_{M_{n}(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})}}^{\tau_{w}}

is then equivalent to
\alpha_{n}\leq C\overleftarrow{\alpha}_{n}
on
M_{n}(E\otimes F)
.
∎

From Theorem
6.13
and Lemma
6.14
we derive the following corollary. Compare to Proposition
6.7
.

Corollary 6.15
.

Suppose that
E
is a normed operator space and
C\geq 1
. The following are equivalent:

(a)

E
has the
C
-W*CBAP.

(b)

C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime}}
is
\tau_{w}
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,E^{\prime\prime})}
.

(c)

For any operator space
F
,
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}}
is
\tau_{w}
-dense in
B_{\CB(E,F^{\prime})}
.

Proof.

The equivalence between
(a)
and
(c)
follows from Theorem
6.13

(c)
and Lemma
6.14
.
Clearly,
(c)
implies
(b)
.
Assuming
(b)
, any
T\in B_{\CB(E,E^{\prime\prime})}
is the
\tau_{w}
-limit of a net in
C\cdot B_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime\prime}}
, which means
T
is also the point-weak limit of the same net (by an argument analogous to that of Remark
6.1
), and hence also the point-weak* limit. Now Proposition
6.7
implies
(a)
.
∎

7.
Mapping ideals

We introduce now the definition of a mapping ideal, the operator space version of Banach operator ideals defined by Pietsch and also widely developed by Defant and Floret.
The notion in our framework comes from
[
ER00
, Sec. 12.2]
, though we have to strengthen Effros and Ruan’s definition in order to keep the essence of the traditional Banach space concept and allow the natural relationship with tensor norms.
As in
[
DF93
]
the definition of ideal is restricted to the category of complete spaces. Let us denote by
\OBAN
the class of all Banach operator spaces.

Definition 7.1
.

A
(normed) mapping ideal

(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is an assignment, for each pair of operator spaces
E,F\in\OBAN
, of a linear space
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)\subseteq\CB(E,F)
together with an operator space structure
\mathbf{A}
on
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
such that

(a)

The identity map
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)\to\CB(E,F)
is a complete contraction.

(b)

For every
x^{\prime}\in M_{n}(E^{\prime})
and
y\in M_{m}(F)
the mapping
x^{\prime}\otimes y
belongs to
M_{nm}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
and
\mathbf{A}_{nm}(x^{\prime}\otimes y)=\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(F)}
.

(c)

The ideal property: whenever
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
,
r\in\CB(E_{0},E)
and
s\in\CB(F,F_{0})
, it follows that
s_{n}\circ T\circ r
belongs to
M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E_{0},F_{0}))
with

\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)\leq\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)\left\|r\right\|_{\cb}.

Remark 7.2
.

Item (b) of the previous definition is the
new
requirement, not considered in
[
ER00
, Sec. 12.2]
.
In the Banach space realm, the definition of normed operator ideal includes that the ideal norm of the identity
id_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
is one
[
Pie67
, Definition 6.1.1.]
. This requisite is in fact the key to relate normed ideals and finitely generated tensor norms.
Unfortunately there is no immediate translation of this requirement into the operator space framework, but nevertheless there is a way to tackle this issue.
By
[
Pie80
, Proposition 6.1.5]
the aforementioned condition about the norm of the identity (together with the other properties of normed operator ideals) implies that —and in fact it is equivalent to— the ideal norm of any elementary tensor
x^{\prime}\otimes y
(for
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y\in F
) is exactly
\|x^{\prime}\|\|y\|
. This has inspired item (b) on our definition. Note the similarity between this statement and equation (
2.4
) in the definition of o.s. tensor norms.

Let us show now that (b) can be somehow relaxed.

First, note that since
\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|_{cb}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(F)}
, condition (a) implies that
\mathbf{A}_{nm}(x^{\prime}\otimes y)\geq\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(F)}
. Hence, we can replace in (b) the symbol
=
by
\leq
.

Also, it is enough that this inequality is satisfied for every
E,F\in\OFIN
. Indeed, let us show that the inequality is valid for every Banach operator space when it is so for every finite-dimensional operator space. Let
x^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{ij})\in M_{n}(E^{\prime})
and
y=(y_{ij})\in M_{m}(F)
with
E,F\in\OBAN
. Consider
L=\bigcap_{i,j}\ker(x^{\prime}_{ij})\in\OCOFIN(E)
and
u^{\prime}\in M_{n}((E/L)^{\prime})
such that
x^{\prime}=(q_{L}^{E})^{\prime}_{n}(u^{\prime})
. Since
(q_{L}^{E})^{\prime}
is a complete isometry, we have
\|u^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}((E/L)^{\prime})}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}
. Choosing
N\in\OFIN(F)
such that
y\in M_{m}(N)
by means of condition (c) of the definition we obtain

\mathbf{A}_{nm}(x^{\prime}\otimes y;E,F)\leq\mathbf{A}_{nm}(u^{\prime}\otimes y;E/L,N)\leq\|u^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}((E/L)^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(N)}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(F)}.

Therefore, we can exchange condition (b) of the previous definition by the following:

(b’)

For every
E,F\in\OFIN
,
x^{\prime}\in M_{n}(E^{\prime})
and
y\in M_{m}(F)
the mapping
x^{\prime}\otimes y
belongs to
M_{nm}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
and
\mathbf{A}_{nm}(x^{\prime}\otimes y)\leq\|x^{\prime}\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\|y\|_{M_{m}(F)}
.

Of course, the class of completely bounded mappings
\CB
is obviously a mapping ideal.

Let us recall the definitions of some other mapping ideals that have already appeared in the literature. We explain in each case (whenever it is not obvious) why condition (b’) is satisfied. Let
E
and
F
be Banach operator spaces.

(i)

Finite rank mappings

\mathcal{F}
:
the space
\mathcal{F}(E,F)
consists of finite rank mappings from
E
to
F
. This is a mapping ideal with the
\cb
-norm.

(ii)

Completely approximable mappings

\mathcal{A}
: the space
\mathcal{A}(E,F)
is the closure of
\mathcal{F}(E,F)
inside
\CB(E,F).

(iii)

Completely nuclear mappings

\mathcal{N}

[
ER00
, 12.2]
: the space
\mathcal{N}(E,F)
is the image of the mapping

(7.1)

\Phi:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F\to E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\subset\CB(E,F)

with the quotient operator space structure given by the canonical identification

\mathcal{N}(E,F)=\frac{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F}{\ker\Phi}.

The nuclear operator space norm is denoted by
\nu
. Equation (
2.4
) for the
\proj
norm shows that (b’) is satisfied for
\mathcal{N}
.

(iv)

Completely integral mappings

\mathcal{I}

[
ER00
, 12.3]
: the space
\mathcal{I}(E,F)
is formed by those mappings such that

\iota(T)=\sup\{(\nu(T|_{M}):M\in\OFIN(E)\}<\infty.

Given
T=(T_{ij})\in M_{n}(\mathcal{I}(E,F))
it is defined

\iota_{n}(T)=\sup\{(\nu_{n}(T|_{M}):M\in\OFIN(E)\}.

This gives an operator space structure on
\mathcal{I}(E,F)
. Since
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{N}
coincide on
\OFIN
, condition (b’) is satisfied for
\mathcal{I}
.

(v)

Completely
p
-summing
\Pi_{p}

[
Pis98
, Chapter 5]
: For
1\leq p<\infty
, a linear map
T:E\to F
belongs to
\Pi_{p}(E,F)
if the mapping

i_{S_{p}}\otimes T:S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E\to S_{p}[F]

is bounded (equivalently, completely bounded), and we denote its norm by
\pi_{p}(T)
. The operator space structure of
\Pi_{p}(E,F)
is inherited from
\CB(S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E,S_{p}[F])
.
As a consequence of
[
CD16
, Thm. 3.1]
for any
E,F\in\OFIN
we have the following complete isometries:

\Pi_{p}(E,F)=(E\otimes_{d_{p^{\prime}}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}=E^{\prime}\otimes_{(d_{p^{\prime}})^{\prime}}F.

Equation (
2.4
) for the
(d_{p^{\prime}})^{\prime}
norm shows that (b’) is satisfied for
\Pi_{p}
.

(vi)

Completely right
p
-nuclear
\mathcal{N}^{p}

[
CDDG19
, Definition 2.1]
: We say that a linear mapping
T:E\to F
belongs to
\mathcal{N}^{p}(E,F)
if it is in the range of the canonical inclusion

J^{p}:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{d_{p}}F\to E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\subset\CB(E,F).

We endow
\mathcal{N}^{p}(E,F)
with the operator space quotient structure
E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{d_{p}}F/KerJ^{p}.
Equation (
2.4
) for the
d_{p}
norm shows that (b’) is satisfied for
\mathcal{N}^{p}
.

(vii)

Operator
p
-compact mappings
\mathcal{K}_{p}

[
CDDG19
, Definition 3.2]
: A mapping
T\in\mathcal{K}_{p}(E,F)
if there exist
G\in\OBAN
, a completely right
p
-nuclear mapping
\Theta\in\mathcal{N}^{p}(G,F)
and a completely bounded mapping
R\in\CB(E,G/\ker\Theta)
with
\|R\|_{\cb}\leq 1
such that the following diagram commutes

(7.2)

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{G/\ker\Theta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Theta}}

where
\pi
and
\widetilde{\Theta}
stand for the natural quotient mapping and the natural monomorphism associated to
\Theta
respectively.

For
T\in\mathcal{K}_{p}(E,F)
, we also define

\kappa_{p}(T):=\inf\{\nu^{p}(\Theta)\},

where the infimum runs over all possible completely right
p
-nuclear mappings
\Theta\in\mathcal{N}^{p}(G,F)
as in (
7.2
).
It should be mentioned
[
CDDG19
, Prop. 3.8.]
that
\mathcal{K}_{p}
is exactly the surjective hull of
\mathcal{N}^{p}
(see Proposition
7.12
below). This provides the operator space structure of
\mathcal{K}_{p}
and shows that condition (b’) is satisfied for this class.

(viii)

Completely
p
-nuclear mappings
\mathcal{N}_{p}

[
Jun96
]
:

A linear map
u:E\to F
is
completely
p
-nuclear
(denoted
u\in\mathcal{N}_{p}(E,F)
) if there exists a factorization of
u
as

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{S_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(a,b)}
\textstyle{S_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{F}

where
a,b\in S_{2p}
,
M(a,b)
is the multiplication operator
x\mapsto axb
, and
\alpha,\beta
completely bounded maps. The completely
p
-nuclear norm of
u
is defined as

\nu_{p}(u)=\inf\big\{\left\|\alpha\right\|_{\cb}\left\|a\right\|_{S_{2p}}\left\|b\right\|_{S_{2p}}\left\|\beta\right\|_{\cb}\big\}

where the infimum is taken over all factorizations of
u
as above.
From
[
Jun96
, Cor. 3.1.3.9 ]
,
when
E,F\in\OBAN

and
1\leq p<\infty
, trace duality yields an isometric isomorphism between
\mathcal{N}_{p}(E,F)^{\prime}
and
\Pi_{p^{\prime}}(F,E^{\prime\prime})
.
This allows us to endow
\mathcal{N}_{p}(E,F)
with an operator space structure. Also, condition (b’) holds since for
E,F\in\OFIN
,

\mathcal{N}_{p}(E,F)=F\otimes_{d_{p}}E^{\prime}
.

(ix)

Completely
(q,p)
-mixing mappings
\mathfrak{M}_{q,p}

[
Yew08
,
CD12
]
:
Let
1\leq p\leq q<\infty
. A mapping
u:E\to F
is said to be
completely
(q,p)
-mixing
(denoted
u\in\mathfrak{M}_{q,p}(E,F)
) if there exists a constant
K
such that for any
G\in\OBAN
and any completely
q
-summing map
v:F\to G
, the composition
v\circ u
is a completely
p
-summing map and
\pi_{p}(v\circ u)\leq K\pi_{q}(v)
. The
completely
(q,p)
-mixing norm
of
u
is the smallest such
K
and is denoted by
\mathfrak{m}_{q,p}(u)
.
By
[
CD12
, Thm. 4.3]
, when
F\subseteq B(H)
we have that
u\in\mathfrak{M}_{q,p}(E,F)
if and only if
for all
n
and all
(x_{ij})
in
M_{n}(E)
we have

\sup\left\{\left\|\big(a(ux_{ij})b\big)\right\|_{S_{p}^{n}[S_{q}(H)]}\;:\;a,b\in B_{S_{2q}(H)},\;a,b\geq 0\right\}\leq C\left\|(x_{ij})\right\|_{S_{p}^{n}\otimes_{\min}E},

so in this way
\mathfrak{M}_{q,p}(E,F)
inherits an operator space structure from
\CB(S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E,\ell_{\infty}(S_{p}[S_{q}(H)]))
.
To verify condition (b’), suppose
E,F\in\OFIN
,
x^{\prime}\in M_{n}(E^{\prime})
and
y\in M_{m}(F)
.
Let
(x_{ij})\in M_{k}(E)
, and consider
a,b\in B_{S_{2q}(H)}
.
Observe that for each
i,j
we have
a\big((x^{\prime}\otimes y)x_{ij}\big)b=(x^{\prime}\otimes ayb)(x_{ij})
.
Now, since we have already observed that condition (b’) is satisfied for the completely
p
-summing maps, the norm of
x^{\prime}\otimes ayb
in
M_{nm}(\CB(S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E,S_{p}[S_{q}(H)]))
is at most
\left\|x^{\prime}\right\|_{M_{n}(E^{\prime})}\left\|ayb\right\|_{M_{m}(S_{q}(H))}
.
Since the multiplication map
w\mapsto awb
from
\mathcal{B}(H)
to
S_{q}(H)
has
\cb
-norm at most
\left\|a\right\|_{S_{2q}}\left\|b\right\|_{S_{2q}}

[
Pis98
, Prop. 5.6]
, taking the supremum over
a,b
yields condition (b’) for
\mathfrak{M}_{q,p}
.

Naturally, the list above is not meant to be exhaustive.
Without going into the details, we point out that
[
Jun96
]
defines a number of other potential examples of mapping ideals including:
completely
p
-integral mappings (Def. 3.1.3.2)
the completely injective hull of the completely
p
-nuclear mappings (Def. 3.1.3.5),
completely
(p,k)
-mappings and
(p,k)
-integral maps (Def. 3.1.3.6),
mappings admitting completely bounded factorizations through diagonal operators from
\ell_{p}
to
\ell_{q}
and their noncommutative analogue, namely two-sided multiplication operators from
S_{p}
to
S_{q}
(Sec. 3.1.4).
While initially only a norm is defined, characterizations are proved that allow us to obtain o.s. structures.
Observe that by Proposition
7.7
, the aforementioned completely injective hull of the completely
p
-nuclear mappings is indeed a mapping ideal.
Note that this is an operator space version of the classical ideal of quasi
p
-nuclear operators (often denoted
\mathcal{QN}_{p}
) introduced in
[
PP69
]
and further studied in e.g.
[
Reĭ82
,
DPnS10
]
.
For all the other aforementioned examples from
[
Jun96
]
we have not verified that condition (b’) is satisfied, but expect that to be the case.

The following definition concerns the completeness of
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
.

Definition 7.3
.

A mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}
is called a
Banach mapping ideal
if
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
is a Banach space, for every
E,F\in\OBAN
.

Note that all the examples listed above are Banach mapping ideals, except for the ideal of finite rank mappings.

As in the Banach space setting, each o.s. tensor norm allows us to construct a mapping ideal “dual” to it.

Example 7.4
.

Given an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
, for
E,F\in\OBAN
let

\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha}(E,F):=(E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F).

Then,
\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha}
is a mapping ideal.

Indeed, the complete contraction
E\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow E\otimes_{\alpha}F^{\prime}
implies that the identity map
\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha}(E,F)\subset\CB(E,F)
is completely contractive too. Also, for
E,F\in\OFIN
,
\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha}(E,F)=E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F
and therefore condition (b’) of the definition holds. On the other hand, the metric mapping property of
\alpha
readily implies the ideal property of
\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha}
.

We now consider ideals that “do not change when followed by a complete isometry”. To be precise:

Definition 7.5
.

A mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}
is said to be
completely injective
if for each complete isometry
i:F\to G
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
the following equivalence holds:
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
if and only if
i_{n}\circ T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,G))
, with
\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T)
.

It straightforward that the mapping ideal
\CB
is completely injective. As a consequence we also have that
\Pi_{p}
is completely injective. Indeed, let
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
and
i:F\to G
a complete isometry such that
i_{n}\circ T\in M_{n}(\Pi_{p}(E,G))
.
Since

M_{n}(\Pi_{p}(E,G))\hookrightarrow M_{n}(\CB(S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E,S_{p}[G]))

and
i
induces a complete isometry from
S_{p}[F]
to
S_{p}[G]

[
Pis98
, Cor. 1.2]
, we obtain that
T\in M_{n}(\Pi_{p}(E,F))
.

Remark 7.6
.

Observe that to prove that a mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}
is completely injective it is enough to check the following two conditions:

(a)

for each complete isometry
i:F\to G
and
T\in\CB(E,F)
such that
i\circ T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,G)
we have that
T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
.

(b)

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T)
for every complete isometry
i:F\to G
and
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
.

A normed operator space
E
has the
complete
C
-extension property
if for every
T\in\CB(E,F)
and a complete injection
i:E\to G
there is
\widetilde{T}\in\CB(G,F)
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{T}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F}

with
\|\widetilde{T}\|_{\cb}\leq C\|T\|_{\cb}.

Recall that
B(H)
has the complete
1
-extension property
[
Pis03
, Thm. 1.6]
.

Given a mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
, the following proposition allows us to talk about the smallest completely injective ideal that contains it.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. Then there is a (unique) smallest completely injective mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}
which contains
\mathfrak{A}
(called the
completely injective hull
of
\mathfrak{A}
). Moreover, if
V
is a completely injective operator space and
i:F\to V
is a complete isometry, then
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
is in
M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F))
if and only if
i_{n}\circ T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,V))
with

\mathbf{A}^{\inj}_{n}(T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T).

Proof.

Given
F\in\OBAN
we denote by
i_{F}:F\hookrightarrow B(H_{F})
any embedding that provides
F
the operator space structure. We define

\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F):=\{T\in\CB(E,F):i_{F}\circ T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,B(H_{F}))\},

endowed with the operator space norm defined for
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
as

\mathbf{A}^{\inj}_{n}(T):=\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T).

To see that this is well defined, let us prove that if
i:F\to V
is any complete isometry of
F
into a completely injective space

V
(which has to be complete as explained in Section
2.5
) then
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
such that
i_{n}\circ T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,V))
we have the equality

(7.3)

\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T).

Indeed, by the injectivity of
B(H_{F})
there is a mapping
\widetilde{i}_{F}:V\to B(H_{F})
such that
i_{F}=\widetilde{i}_{F}\circ i
with
\|\widetilde{i}_{F}\|_{\cb}=1
.
Then, by the ideal property

\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}((\widetilde{i}_{F})_{n}\circ i_{n}\circ T)\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T).

On the other hand, by the the injectivity of
V
, we can argue as before to show the existence of a mapping
\widetilde{i}:B(H_{F})\to V
such that

i=\widetilde{i}\circ i_{F}
with
\|\widetilde{i}\|_{\cb}=1
and therefore

\mathbf{A}_{n}(i_{n}\circ T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}((\widetilde{i})_{n}\circ(i_{F})_{n}\circ T)\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T).

This shows Equation (
7.3
).

To see that
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}
is a mapping ideal, we will only prove the ideal property since the other conditions easily follow.
Let
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F))
,
r\in\CB(E_{0},E)
and
s\in\CB(F,F_{0})
.
Then,

\mathbf{A}^{\inj}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)=\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F_{0}})_{n}\circ s_{n}\circ T\circ r)\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F_{0}}\circ s)_{n}\circ T)\|r\|_{\cb}

Once again the injectivity of
B(H_{F_{0}})
provides us with a mapping
\widetilde{s}\in\CB(B(H_{F}),B(H_{F_{0}}))
satisfying

i_{F_{0}}\circ s=\widetilde{s}\circ i_{F}
, with
\|\widetilde{s}\|_{\cb}\leq\|s\|_{\cb}
. Thus,

\mathbf{A}^{\inj}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}((\widetilde{s}\circ i_{F})_{n}\circ T)\|r\|_{\cb}\leq\|s\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T)\|r\|_{\cb}=\|s\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\inj}(T)\|r\|_{\cb}.

We now see that
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}
is completely injective. Indeed, let
i:F\to G
a complete isometry,
T\in\CB(E,F)
and suppose that
i\circ T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,G)
. If
i_{G}:G\to B(H_{G})
is a completely isometric embedding, the extension property of
B(H_{G})
shows the existence of a mapping
\widetilde{i_{F}}
such that

(7.4)

\widetilde{i_{F}}\circ(i_{G}\circ i)=i_{F}.

Now, the definition of the ideal implies
i_{G}\circ i\circ T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,B(H_{G}))
. Then,
\widetilde{i_{F}}\circ(i_{G}\circ i)\circ T=i_{F}\circ T
belongs to
\mathfrak{A}(E,B(H_{F}))
. Hence,
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F).

Now, if
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F))
and
i:F\to G
is a complete isometry, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\inj}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{F})_{n}\circ T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}((\widetilde{i_{F}}\circ i_{G}\circ i)_{n}\circ T)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}((i_{G})_{n}\circ(i_{n}\circ T))=\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\inj}(i_{n}\circ T).

That
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}
is the smallest completely injective ideal which contains
\mathfrak{A}
is trivial by definition of
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}
. The last part of the statement follows from what was done at the beginning of the proof to show that
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(T)
was well defined.
∎

Example 7.8
.

For the mapping ideal
\mathcal{I}
of completely integral mappings, the previous proposition says that
T\in\mathcal{I}^{\inj}(E,F)
if and only if
i_{F}\circ T\in\mathcal{I}(E,B(H_{F}))
. By
[
ER00
, Prop. 15.5.1]

\mathcal{I}(E,B(H_{F}))=\Pi_{1}(E,B(H_{F}))
.
Since we have already explained that
\Pi_{1}
is a completely injective mapping ideal we derive that
\mathcal{I}^{\inj}=\Pi_{1}
(as it happens in the Banach space framework).

The following simple result shows that the
\inj
-procedure respects completeness.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj},\mathbf{A}^{\inj})
is also a Banach mapping ideal.

Proof.

Let
E,F\in\OBAN
, we only have to check that
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F)
is complete.
Let
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F)
. Note that
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is also a Cauchy sequence in
\CB(E,F)
and then it converges (in the
cb
-norm) to an operator
T\in\CB(E,F)
.
On the other hand,
(i_{F}\circ T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathfrak{A}(E,B(H_{F}))
which converges to an operator
S\in\mathfrak{A}(E,B(H_{F}))
. To finish, it remains to prove that
S=i_{F}\circ T
. Indeed, note that
T_{k}\to T
pointwise and therefore
i_{F}\circ T_{k}\to i_{F}\circ T
pointwise as well. Now the result follows by the uniqueness of the limit.

∎

As a corollary we have an analogous version of
[
DF93
, Corollary 9.7]

for the operator space setting.

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
F\in\OBAN
with the complete
C
-extension property and
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every
E\in\OBAN
we have

M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))=M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F))
. Moreover,

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)\leq C\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\inj}(T),

for all
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\inj}(E,F))
.

Similarly, we can consider ideals that behave well when composed with complete metric surjections.

Definition 7.11
.

A mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is said to be
completely surjective
if for each complete metric surjection
q:G\twoheadrightarrow E
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
the following equivalence holds:
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
if and only if
T\circ q\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(G,F))
, with
\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q)
.

It is immediate to see that the mapping ideal
\CB
is completely surjective.

Given a mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
, the following proposition defines the smallest completely surjective ideal that contains it.
This was already proved in
[
CDDG19
, Prop. 3.6]
, we include a detailed proof for completeness.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. Then there is a (unique) smallest completely surjective mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}
which contains
\mathfrak{A}
(called the
completely surjective hull
of
\mathfrak{A}
). Moreover: if
G
is a completely projective Banach operator space,
E\in\OBAN
and

q:G\twoheadrightarrow E
is a complete metric surjection, then
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
is in
M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F))
if and only if
T\circ q\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(G,F))
with

\mathbf{A}^{\sur}_{n}(T)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q).

Proof.

We define

\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F)=\{T\in\CB(E,F):T\circ q_{E}\in\mathfrak{A}(Z_{E},F)\},

endowed with the operator space structure defined in the statement.
First we see that it is well defined. To see this, suppose that
G
is a completely projective Banach operator space and

q:G\twoheadrightarrow E
is a complete metric surjection and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
such that
T\circ q\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(G,F))
, we have to see that

(7.5)

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q_{E})=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q).

By the complete projectivity of
Z_{E}
there is a mapping
\widetilde{q}_{E}:Z_{E}\to G
such that
q_{E}=q\circ\widetilde{q}_{E}
with
\|\widetilde{q}_{E}\|_{\cb}\leq 1
. Then,

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q_{E})=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q\circ\widetilde{q}_{E})\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q).

On the other hand, by the projectivity of
G
there is a mapping
\widetilde{q}:G\to Z_{E}
such that
q=q_{E}\circ\widetilde{q}
with
\|\widetilde{q}\|_{\cb}\leq 1.
As before,

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q_{E}\circ\widetilde{q})\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}(T\circ q_{E}).

This shows Equation (
7.5
).

To see that
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}
is a mapping ideal, we will only prove the ideal property since the other conditions easily follow.

Let
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F))
,
r\in\CB(E_{0},E)
and
s\in\CB(F,F_{0})
.
Since
Z_{E_{0}}
is completely projective, given
\varepsilon>0
, there is a lifting
L_{\varepsilon}\in\CB(Z_{E_{0}},Z_{E})
of
r\circ q_{E_{0}}
with
\|L_{\varepsilon}\|_{\cb}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|r\|_{\cb}
such that the following diagram commutes

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.6109pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.22221pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 80.39728pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.39728pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.4511pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.21674pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 118.21674pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{0}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-12.10004pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z_{E_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.6109pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.25418pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q_{E_{0}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower 3.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.44463pt\raise-37.22498pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{L_{\varepsilon}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.10004pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 36.10004pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z_{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.24866pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q_{E}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.24866pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower 3.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 84.307pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 124.36952pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Then, for every
1\leq i,j\leq n
we have
s\circ T_{ij}\circ r\circ q_{E_{0}}=s\circ T_{ij}\circ q_{E}\circ L_{\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{A}(Z_{E_{0}},F_{0})
and hence
s\circ T_{ij}\circ r\in\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E_{0},F_{0})
.
Moreover, by the ideal property of
\mathfrak{A}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\sur}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)
