\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r\circ q_{E_{0}})=\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ q_{E}\circ L_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ q_{E})(1+\varepsilon)\|r\|_{\cb}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|s\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\sur}(T)\|r\|_{\cb}.

We now prove that the mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}
is surjective. Let
q:G\twoheadrightarrow E
be a complete quotient mapping and
T\in\CB(E,F)
such that
T\circ q\in\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(G,F)
.
Since
Z_{E}
is completely projective and
q\circ q_{G}:Z_{G}\twoheadrightarrow E
is a complete quotient mapping, given
\varepsilon>0
, there is a lifting
L_{\varepsilon}:Z_{E}\to Z_{G}
of
q_{E}
, with cb-norm less than or equal to
1+\varepsilon
.

We have
T\circ q_{E}=T\circ q\circ q_{G}\circ L_{\varepsilon}\in\mathfrak{A}(Z_{E},F)
since
T\circ q\circ q_{G}\in\mathfrak{A}(Z_{G},F)
. Then,
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F)
.
Also, if
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F))
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\sur}(T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}(T\circ q_{E})=\mathbf{A}(T\circ q\circ q_{G}\circ L_{\varepsilon})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}(T\circ q\circ q_{G})

\displaystyle=(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\sur}(T\circ q)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\sur}(T).

That
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}
is the smallest completely projective ideal is trivial by definition of
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}
. The last part of the statement follows from what was done at the beginning of the proof to show that
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(T)
was well defined.
∎

It was proven in
[
CDDG19
, Prop. 3.8]
that

\mathcal{K}_{p}=(\mathcal{N}^{p})^{\sur}.

As a consequence, we obviously have that
\mathcal{K}_{p}
is a surjective mapping ideal.

As a corollary of the previous proposition we have an analogous version of
[
DF93
, Corollary 9.8]

for the operator space setting.

Corollary 7.13
.

Let
E
be a completely projective Banach operator space and
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. Then for every every
F\in\OBAN
we have that

\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
and
\mathfrak{A}^{\sur}(E,F)
are completely isometric.

As for the
\inj
-procedure, we have that the
\sur
-procedure also respects completeness. The proof is analogous to that of Proposition
7.9
so we omit it.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\sur},\mathbf{A}^{\sur})
is also a Banach mapping ideal.

We now define the dual procedure of a mapping ideal.

Definition 7.15
.

If
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is a mapping ideal, define for
E,F\in\OBAN

\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}(E,F):=\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}(F^{\prime},E^{\prime})\}

and

\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\dual}(T):=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T^{\prime}),\quad T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}(E,F)).

It is readily seen that
(\mathfrak{A}^{\dual},\mathbf{A}^{\dual})
is also a mapping ideal.

It is clear from the definition that the ideal of completely approximable mappings satisfies that
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}
. On the other hand, by
[
ER00
, Proposition 12.2.5]
, if
T\in\mathcal{N}(E,F)
we have that
T\in\mathcal{N}^{\dual}(E,F)
with
\nu^{\dual}(T)=\nu(T^{\prime})\leq\nu(T).

In fact, more is true: the same proof shows that the map
\mathcal{N}(E,F)\to\mathcal{N}(F^{\prime},E^{\prime})
given by
T\mapsto T^{\prime}
is a complete contraction,
and thus the identity map
\mathcal{N}(E,F)\to\mathcal{N}^{\dual}(E,F)
is a complete contraction as well.
In the case when
E^{\prime}
has the operator approximation property
the map
\Phi
in (
7.1
) is injective
[
ER00
, Thm. 11.2.5]
,
and therefore the same proof as in
[
ER00
, Proposition 12.2.6]
shows that the two maps above are in fact complete isometries.

Let us also mention that mappings belonging to the ideals
(\Pi_{p})^{\dual}
have already been considered in the literature.
For example,
[
Pis98
, Thm. 6.5]
characterizes mappings that admit a completely bounded factorization through Pisier’s operator Hilbert space
OH
in terms of conditions involving both
\Pi_{2}
and
(\Pi_{2})^{\dual}
,
and
[
Pis98
, Cor. 7.2.2]
similarly characterizes mappings admitting a completely bounded factorization through finite-dimensional Schatten
p
-spaces with conditions that involve
\Pi_{p^{\prime}}
and
(\Pi_{p})^{\dual}
.

8.
Maximal o.s. mapping ideals

In the Banach space setting there is an intrinsic relation between tensor norms and operator ideals.
As mentioned before, the same happens in the non-commutative context. In this section we develop the theory of maximal mapping ideals, showing that the latter and the theory of o.s. tensor norms are two sides of the same coin.

If
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is a mapping ideal, then

M\otimes_{\alpha}N:=\mathfrak{A}(M^{\prime},N)

defines an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
on
\OFIN
; in other words, if
z\in M_{n}(M\otimes N)
and
T_{z}\in M_{n}(\CB(M^{\prime},N))
is the associated matrix of linear operators, then

\alpha_{n}(z;M,N):=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T_{z}:M^{\prime}\to N).

Since the natural maps
\mathfrak{A}(M^{\prime},N)\to\CB(M^{\prime},N)
and
M\otimes_{\min}N\to\CB(M^{\prime},N)
are respectively a complete contraction and a complete isometry, it follows that

M\otimes_{\alpha}N\to M\otimes_{\min}N

is a complete contraction.

Note that equation (
2.4
) follows immediately from condition (b) in the definition of mapping ideals. Also,
the ideal property of
\mathfrak{A}
implies the complete metric mapping property of
\alpha
:
for any linear maps
r\in\CB(M,M_{0})
and
s\in\CB(N,N_{0})
, note that for any
z\in M_{n}(M\otimes N)

s_{n}\circ T_{z}\circ r^{\prime}=T_{(r\otimes s)(z)}.

and thus

\alpha_{n}\big((r\otimes s)(z)\big)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(T_{(r\otimes s)(z)})=\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T_{z}\circ r^{\prime})\leq\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}(T_{z})\left\|r^{\prime}\right\|_{\cb}=\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\left\|r\right\|_{\cb}\alpha_{n}(z).

Conversely, let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm on
\OFIN
satisfying the complete metric mapping property.
Define for
M,N\in\OFIN
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(M,N))

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T:M\to N):=\alpha_{n}(z_{T};M^{\prime},N).

where
z_{T}\in M_{n}(M^{\prime}\otimes N)
is the associated matrix of tensors.
Let
r\in\CB(M_{0},M)
and
s\in CB(N,N_{0})
. Again, condition (b) is related with equation (
2.4
).
Note that

z_{s_{n}\circ T\circ r}=\big(r^{\prime}\otimes s\big)z_{T}

and thus, by the complete metric mapping property of
\alpha
,

\mathbf{A}_{n}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)=\alpha_{n}(z_{s_{n}\circ T\circ r})\leq\left\|r^{\prime}\right\|_{\cb}\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\alpha_{n}(z_{T})=\left\|r\right\|_{\cb}\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)

Therefore, in the finite-dimensional setting there is a full correspondence between mapping ideals and o.s. tensor norms. The rest of this section is devoted to the study of what can be said in the infinite-dimensional setting.
Naturally, the answer is satisfactory for mapping ideals that can be well-approximated by finite-dimensional pieces, the idea that motivates the next definition.

Definition 8.1
.

Assume
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is a mapping ideal. For
E,F\in\OBAN
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
, define

\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{n}(T)=\sup\left\{\mathbf{A}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})\colon M\in\OFIN(E),L\in\OCOFIN(F)\right\}

and

\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)=\left\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}(T)<\infty\right\}.

We call
(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
the
maximal hull
of
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
, and

(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is called
maximal
if
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})=(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. Then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
is a Banach mapping ideal which, of course, is maximal.

Proof.

It is clear that each
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{n}
is a norm, and that
\mathbf{A}^{\max}
satisfies Ruan’s axioms (say, as in
[
ER00
, Prop. 2.3.6]
).

Using that the identity
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)\to\CB(E,F)
is a complete contraction, and the ideal property for the
\cb
-norm, for
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
we have that

\mathbf{A}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})\leq\left\|(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}\right\|_{M_{n}(\CB(M,F/L))}\leq\left\|T\right\|_{M_{n}(\CB(E,F))},

which implies that the identity
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)\to\CB(E,F)
is also a complete contraction.
Similarly, the ideal property for
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
shows that all finite-rank operators belong to
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}
since they belong to
\mathfrak{A}
. Moreover, given
x^{\prime}\in M_{n}(E^{\prime})
and
y\in M_{m}(F)
we have

\mathbf{A}_{nm}((q^{F}_{L})_{nm}\circ x^{\prime}\otimes y\circ i^{E}_{M})=\mathbf{A}_{nm}(x^{\prime}\circ i^{E}_{M}\otimes(q^{F}_{L})_{m}(y))=\|x^{\prime}\circ i^{E}_{M}\|_{\cb}\|(q^{F}_{L})_{m}(y)\|_{\cb},

showing that
\mathbf{A}_{nm}^{\max}(x^{\prime}\otimes y)\leq\|x^{\prime}\|_{\cb}\|y\|_{\cb}
.

Suppose now that
r\in\CB(E_{0},E)
and
s\in\CB(F,F_{0})
.
Let
M_{0}\in\OFIN(E_{0})
and
L_{0}\in\OCOFIN(F_{0})
.
Define
M=r(M_{0})\in\OFIN(E)
and
L=s^{-1}(L_{0})\in\OCOFIN(F)
.
Note that
r=i^{E}_{M}\circ r
, and
q^{F_{0}}_{L_{0}}\circ s=\widetilde{s}\circ q^{F}_{L}
for some
\widetilde{s}\in\CB(F/L,F_{0}/L_{0})
with
\|\widetilde{s}\|_{\cb}=\|{s}\|_{\cb}
.
Therefore, by the ideal property for
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})

\mathbf{A}_{n}\big((q^{F_{0}}_{L_{0}})_{n}\circ s_{n}\circ T\circ r\circ i^{E_{0}}_{M_{0}}\big)=\mathbf{A}_{n}\big(\widetilde{s}_{n}\circ(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}\circ r\circ i^{E_{0}}_{M_{0}}\big)\\
\leq\|\widetilde{s}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}\big((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}\big)\|r\circ i^{E_{0}}_{M_{0}}\|_{\cb}\leq\|s\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{n}(T)\|r\|_{\cb},

which yields the ideal property for
(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
.

Let us now check that
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)
is complete when
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is a mapping ideal.
For that, let
(T_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence in
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}(T_{k})<\infty
. Since
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}
dominates the
\cb
-norm, the series
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}T_{k}
converges in
\CB(E,F)
to an operator
T
.
Let us now show that the convergence also holds with respect to
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}
.
Fix
M\in\OFIN(E)
and
L\in\OCOFIN(F)
.
For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, note that

\mathbf{A}_{1}\Big(q^{F}_{L}\circ\Big(\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}T_{k}\Big)\circ i^{E}_{M}\Big)\leq\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}_{1}\big(q^{F}_{L}\circ T_{k}\circ i^{E}_{M}\big)\leq\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}(T_{k}),

from where
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}\big(\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}T_{k}\big)\leq\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}(T_{k})
and therefore
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}T_{k}
converges to
T
with respect to
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{1}
.
∎

Example 8.3
.

The following mapping ideals are maximal.

(i)

Completely bounded mappings
\CB
.

(ii)

Completely integral mappings
\mathcal{I}
: See Example
8.8
, below.

(iii)

Completely
p
-summing mappings
\Pi_{p}
: Let
E,F\in\OBAN
,
T\in M_{n}(\Pi_{p}(E,F))
and
{\varepsilon}>0
.
Assume
(\pi_{p})_{n}(T)=1
.
Since
\Pi_{p}(E,F)
is canonically completely isometrically embedded into
\CB(S_{p}\otimes_{\min}E,S_{p}[F])
,
by a density argument there exist
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
x\in M_{n}(S^{k}_{p}\otimes_{\min}E)
with
\left\|x\right\|=1
such that

\left\|(id_{S^{k}_{p}}\otimes T)(x)\right\|_{M_{n}(S^{k}_{p}[F])}>1-\varepsilon,

Since
S^{k}_{p}[F]
is completely isometrically embedded into
S^{k}_{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]=(S_{p^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}])^{\prime}
,
there exists
y\in M_{\ell}(S_{p^{\prime}}^{k}[F^{\prime}])
with
\left\|y\right\|=1
and such that

\left\|{\langle\langle y,(id_{S^{k}_{p}}\otimes T)(x)\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{n\ell}}>1-\varepsilon.

Consider
y
as an
k\ell\times k\ell
matrix over
F^{\prime}
, and let
L
be the intersection of these
(k\ell)^{2}
functionals in
F^{\prime}
.
Note that
L\in\OCOFIN(F)
, and that
y
is an
k\ell\times k\ell
matrix over the annihilator
L^{0}
of
L
in
F
.
Since
(F/L)=L^{0}
completely isometrically, it follows that
S^{k}_{p^{\prime}}[F/L]=S^{k}_{p^{\prime}}[L^{0}]
completely isometrically as well. Therefore, the norm of
y
as an element of
M_{\ell}(S_{p^{\prime}}^{k}[(F/L)^{\prime}])
is also equal to one.
If
M
is the finite-dimensional subspace of
E
spanned by the
(nk)^{2}
vectors that constitute
x
, it follows that

(\pi_{p})_{n}\big((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}\big)>1-\varepsilon.

Definition 8.4
.

A mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
and a finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
(on
\ONORM
) are said to be
associated
, denoted
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})\sim\alpha
if for every
M,N\in\OFIN
we have a complete isometry

\mathfrak{A}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}N,

given by the canonical map
T\mapsto z_{T}
.

Remark 8.5
.

Notice that since this definition is based only on finite-dimensional spaces; two different mapping ideals can be associated to the same o.s. tensor norm. In particular,
\mathfrak{A}\sim\alpha
if and only if
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}\sim\alpha
.

For example,
\CB,\mathcal{A}\sim\min
or
\mathcal{N},\mathcal{I}\sim\proj
.
Also, by the mere definition,
\mathcal{N}_{p}\sim d_{p}
.

Observe that the constructions in Definitions
3.1
and
8.1
establish a one-to-one correspondence between maximal mapping ideals and finitely-generated o.s. tensor norms.
The main result of this section is the following theorem, which shows that the finite-dimensional duality that defines this correspondence can be extended to the infinite-dimensional setting.

Theorem 8.6

(Representation Theorem for maximal mapping ideals)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal associated to the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then for any
E,F\in\OBAN
there is a complete isometry

(8.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}(E,F)

\displaystyle=\left(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)

Proof.

In order to prove (
8.1
), we need to show that for
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
, the following holds:
T
belongs to
M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
if and only if the associated matrix of bilinear maps
\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T}
is in
M_{n}\big((E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\big)
, with equal norms.
Note that the inequality
\left\|\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T}\right\|\leq C
is equivalent to

(8.2)

\left\|{\langle\langle\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T},z\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\leq C(\alpha^{\prime})_{m}(z;E,F^{\prime})\text{ for all }z\in M_{m}(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime}).

By the maximality of
\mathfrak{A}
, it follows that

T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}(E,F))
with
\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)\leq C
if and only if

(8.3)

\mathbf{A}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})\leq C\qquad\text{ for any }M\in\OFIN(E)\text{ and }L\in\OCOFIN(F).

Since
\mathfrak{A}(M,F/L)=(M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}L^{0})^{\prime}
completely isometrically,
and recalling that
L^{0}
varies over all spaces in
\OFIN(F^{\prime})
when
L
varies over all spaces in
\OCOFIN(F)
, condition (
8.3
)
is equivalent to

(8.4)

\left\|{\langle\langle\beta_{(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}},z\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\leq C(\alpha^{\prime})_{m}(z;M,L^{0})\\
\text{ for any }M\in\OFIN(E),\;L^{0}\in\OFIN(F^{\prime})\text{ and }z\in M_{m}(M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}L^{0}).

Notice that for
z\in M_{m}(M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}L^{0})
,

{\langle\langle\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T},z\rangle\rangle}={\langle\langle\beta_{(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M}},z\rangle\rangle}.

Therefore (
8.4
) is equivalent to (
8.2
) because
\alpha^{\prime}
is finitely-generated,
finishing the proof.
∎

In fact the converse is also true. We write the statement in the following proposition and leave the proof as an exercise. Recall that in Example
7.4
we define a mapping ideal “dual” to a given o.s. tensor norm.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
\alpha
be a finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm. The mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha^{\prime}},\mathbf{A}_{\alpha^{\prime}})
(given by

(8.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(E,F)

\displaystyle:=\left(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)

for any
E,F\in\OBAN
) is maximal and its associated o.s. tensor norm is
\alpha.

We use this fact to see, in the following example, that the ideal of completely integral mappings is maximal.

Example 8.8
.

\mathcal{I}
is a maximal mapping ideal.

Proof.

We will give two proofs of this (for the second, we present only a sketch of it). Let’s start with the first one.

Recall that the o.s. tensor norm associated to
\mathcal{I}
is
\proj
. By the previous proposition, to see that
\mathcal{I}
is maximal we need to check that for every
E,F\in\OBAN
there is a complete isometry

(8.6)

\mathcal{I}(E,F)=\left(E\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F).

By
[
ER00
, Lemma 12.3.3]
the canonical inclusion

S_{0}:\mathcal{I}(E,F)\hookrightarrow(E\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}

is a complete isometry. Thus, it remains to see that if
\Psi\in(E\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)
then
\Psi=S_{0}(T)
, for some
T\in\mathcal{I}(E,F).

Without loss of generality, we suppose that
\|\Psi\|_{(E\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime})^{\prime}}<1.

By Remark
5.6
the following inclusion is completely isometric:

E\otimes_{\min}F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime}=\CB(E^{\prime},F^{\prime}).

Then, by the Averson-Wittstock Hahn-Banach theorem
[
ER00
, Theorem 4.1.5]
,
\Psi
extends isometrically to
(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, Goldstine’s theorem provides us of a net
(\Psi_{\gamma})_{\gamma}
such that
\|\Psi_{\gamma}\|_{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F}<1,
which
w^{*}
-converges to
\Psi
(that is in the topology
\sigma((E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime\prime},(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime})
.
We consider
\Phi(\Psi_{\gamma})_{\gamma}\subset\mathcal{N}(E,F)
with
\nu(\Phi(\Psi_{\gamma}))<1
, where
\Phi
is the mapping defined in (
7.1
).
Note that given
x\in E
,
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}

\langle y^{\prime},\Phi(\Psi_{\gamma})\rangle\to\langle\Psi,x\otimes y^{\prime}\rangle.

Now, the expression
\langle T(x),y^{\prime}\rangle:=\langle\Psi,x\otimes y^{\prime}\rangle
defines a mapping
T\in\CB(E,F)
satisfying that
\Phi(\Psi_{\gamma})
converges to
T
in the point-weak topology. Finally, appealing to
[
ER00
, Lemma 12.3.1]
we get that
T
is completely integral and
\Psi=S_{0}(T)
. This concludes the first proof.

We now present a sketch of an alternative argument for the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is maximal,
whose perspective could be enriching.
First, observe that
[
DF93
, Ex. 17.1]
also holds in the operator space setting. That is:
suppose that for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
(\mathfrak{A}^{\gamma},\mathbf{A}^{\gamma})
is a maximal mapping ideal.
Define for
E,F\in\OBAN
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
,

\mathbf{A}_{n}(T)=\sup\left\{\mathbf{A}^{\gamma}_{n}(T)\,:\,\gamma\in\Gamma\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{A}(E,F)=\left\{S\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,\mathbf{A}_{1}(S)<\infty\right\}.

It is easy to see that
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is also a maximal mapping ideal.
Now observe that
[
ER00
, Lemma 12.3.1]
says that the ideal of completely integral mappings can be obtained by applying the above procedure to the ideals
(\mathfrak{A}^{G},\mathbf{A}^{G})
defined by the completely isometric embeddings

\mathfrak{A}^{G}(E,F)\hookrightarrow\CB(E\otimes_{\min}G,F\otimes_{\proj}G),

where
G
ranges over all Banach operator spaces (or just the finite-dimensional ones).
Since each
\mathfrak{A}^{G}
is clearly maximal, it follows that so is
\mathcal{I}
. This concludes the sketch of the second proof.
∎

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
and
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
be Banach mapping ideals. Suppose that for every
M,N\in\OFIN
we have that the inclusion
\|\mathfrak{A}(M,N)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{B}(M,N)\|_{\cb}\leq c
. Then

\mathfrak{A}^{\max}\subset\mathfrak{B}^{\max}
and

\|\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{\max}(E,F)\|_{\cb}\leq c
for every
E,F\in\OBAN
.

Proof.

Let
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
.
Fix
M\in\OFIN(E)
and
L\in\OCOFIN(F)
. From the assumption, note that

\mathbf{B}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})\leq c\mathbf{A}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})
. Taking the supremum over all
M
and
L
yields
\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\max}(T)\leq c\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\max}(T)
, giving the result.
∎

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal associated to a finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
is the largest mapping ideal associated to
\alpha
.

Proof.

The ideal property of
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
yields that the identity
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)\to\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)
is completely contractive for any
E,F\in\OBAN
. For
M,N\in\OFIN
we additionally get that the identity
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(M,N)\to\mathfrak{A}(M,N)
is also completely contractive since for
T\in\CB(M,N)
we have that
T=(q^{N}_{\{0\}})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{M}_{M}
. Therefore, the identity
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(M,N)\to\mathfrak{A}(M,N)
is a complete isomorphism and thus
(\mathfrak{A}^{\max},\mathbf{A}^{\max})
is associated to
\alpha
.

Let
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
be any mapping ideal associated to
\alpha
, and let
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{B}(E,F))
.
Let
M\in\OFIN(E)
and
L\in\OCOFIN(F)
. By the ideal property for
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
we have that

\mathbf{A}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})=\mathbf{B}_{n}((q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M})\leq\mathbf{B}_{n}(T),

from where
\mathbf{A}^{\max}_{n}(T)\leq\mathbf{B}_{n}(T)
, showing that
\mathfrak{B}(E,F)\subseteq\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)
(and in fact the inclusion
\mathfrak{B}(E,F)\to\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(E,F)
is completely contractive).

∎

Remark 8.11
.

Given a finite dimensional operator space
M
and an arbitrary
E\in\OBAN
there is a canonical complete isometry:

M\otimes_{\proj}E\hookrightarrow(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}E^{\prime})^{\prime}.

Indeed, since
M
is finite dimensional we have
M\otimes_{\proj}E=\mathcal{N}(M^{\prime},E)=\mathcal{I}(M^{\prime},E)
. Now, the result follows through the embedding
S_{0}
referred to in Example
8.8
. By transposition, there is also the following complete isometry:

E\otimes_{\proj}M\hookrightarrow(E^{\prime}\otimes_{\min}M^{\prime})^{\prime}.

Another classical version of the Representation Theorem states, in particular, that a maximal ideal taking values in a dual space is itself a dual space. To get the operator space version, we need the additional hypothesis of local reflexivity.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal and
\alpha
its associated o.s. tensor norm.
If
F
is locally reflexive, then

\mathfrak{A}(E,F^{\prime})=(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime}

Proof.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{\varphi\in(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F)^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(E\otimes_{\proj}F)^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\CB(E,F^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\varphi^{\wedge}\in(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(E\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}.}

The vertical arrows are complete isometries thanks to the Extension Lemma
4.3
,
whereas the horizontal arrows are continuous because
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\proj
.
The desired conclusion follows from (
8.5
).
∎

Note that the hypothesis of local reflexivity cannot be omitted from the previous result. Indeed, taking
\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{I}
(the mapping ideal of completely integral mappings) which is associated to the o.s. tensor norm
\proj
, we know by
[
ER00
, Theorem 14.3.1]
that the equality
\mathcal{I}(E,F^{\prime})=(E\otimes_{\min}F)^{\prime}
holds for every
E\in\OBAN
if and only if
F
is locally reflexive.

On the other hand, whenever
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm the Extension Lemma
4.3
is valid without the hypothesis of local reflexivity. Thus, the previous corollary also holds without this hypothesis.

Along similar lines, in this same setting we can get a version of
[
DF93
, Thm. 17.15]
.

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal associated to the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
.
Suppose that
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.
Then for any
E,F\in\OBAN
and
T\in\CB(E,F)
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
.

(b)

For any
G\in\OBAN
(or only
G=F^{\prime}
) the map

T\otimes id_{G}:E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}G\to F\otimes_{\proj}G

is bounded.

In this case,

\mathbf{A}(T)=\left\|T\otimes id_{F^{\prime}}:E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\to F\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime}\right\|\geq\left\|T\otimes id_{G}:E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}G\to F\otimes_{\proj}G\right\|.

Proof.

Suppose that
T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
. Let
\varphi\in(F\otimes_{\proj}G)^{\prime}=\CB(F,G^{\prime})
.
Note that
L_{\varphi}\circ T\in\mathfrak{A}(E,G^{\prime})=(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}G)^{\prime}
, where we are using that the previous corollary holds in general because
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm.
We then have for
z\in E\otimes G
, using
[
DF93
, Formula 17.15.(1)]
,

|{\langle\varphi,(T\otimes id_{G})(z)\rangle}|=|{\langle\beta_{L_{\varphi}\circ T},z\rangle}|\leq\mathbf{A}(L_{\varphi}\circ T)\alpha^{\prime}(z;E,G)\leq\left\|\varphi\right\|\mathbf{A}(T)\alpha^{\prime}(z;E,G).

Taking the supremum over
\left\|\varphi\right\|\leq 1
yields

\proj((T\otimes id_{G})(z);F,G)\leq\mathbf{A}(T)\alpha^{\prime}(z;E,G).

Now, assume that (b) is satisfied for
G=F^{\prime}
.
Recall that from the Representation Theorem,
\mathfrak{A}(E,F)
coincides with
(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)
. Since
T\in\CB(E,F)
, it will suffice to show that

\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T}\in(E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}
.
If we now let
z\in E\otimes F^{\prime}
, using
[
DF93
, Formula 17.15.(2)]
,

|{\langle\beta_{\kappa_{F}\circ T},z\rangle}|=|{\langle\tr_{F},(T\otimes id_{F^{\prime}})(z)\rangle}|\leq\proj((T\otimes id_{F^{\prime}})(z);F,F^{\prime})\\
\leq\alpha^{\prime}(z;E,F^{\prime})\left\|T\otimes id_{G}:E\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}F^{\prime}\to F\otimes_{\proj}F^{\prime}\right\|.

∎

Once we have the theorem above, we get a version of
[
DF93
, Prop. 17.20]
.
Notice that this is a generalization of
[
AK21
, Prop. 4]
, where the same result is obtained for some
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norms.

Proposition 8.14
.

For any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm and
E,F\in\OBAN
, one of which has the OAP, the natural map

i:E\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda}F\to E\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F

is injective.

Proof.

Suppose that
F
has the OAP.
Let
z\in E\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda}F
be such that
i(z)=0
.
Observe that we need to show that
{\langle\varphi,z\rangle}=0
whenever
\varphi\in(E\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda}F)^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\CB(E,F^{\prime})
.

Note that since
\lambda
is finitely generated, we have
\lambda=(\lambda^{\prime})^{\prime}
and therefore the previous theorem applies, so

L_{\varphi}\otimes id_{F}:E\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda}F\to F^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F

is continuous. In the diagram

\textstyle{E\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{\varphi}\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda,\proj}id_{F}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{E\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{\varphi}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}id_{F}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F}

the bottom row is injective because
F
has OAP
[
ER00
, Thm. 11.2.5]
, which implies

(L_{\varphi}\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda,\proj}id_{F})(z)=0\in F^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F.

Once again using using
[
DF93
, Formula 17.15.(2)]
, we get

{\langle\varphi,z\rangle}={\langle\tr F,(L_{\varphi}\widehat{\otimes}_{\lambda,\proj}id_{F})(z)\rangle}=0.

∎

Example 8.15
.

For the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm
h
consider as in Example
7.4
the mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}_{h}
. Then,
\mathfrak{A}_{h}
is a maximal mapping ideal defined as

\mathfrak{A}_{h}(E,F):=(E\otimes_{h}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)

for every
E,F\in\OBAN
.

Since
\alpha^{\prime}=h
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm, we have

\mathfrak{A}_{h}(E,F^{\prime})=(E\otimes_{h}F)^{\prime}

for every
E,F\in\OBAN
.
Also, recall that the dual of
E\otimes_{h}F
is identified with the space
\MB(E\times F)
of multiplicatively bounded bilinear mappings (see, for instance,
[
ER00
, Prop. 9.2.2]
) then we have the following complete isometry:

\mathfrak{A}_{h}(E,F^{\prime})=\MB(E\times F).

Combining the Representation Theorem
8.6
and
8.12
with the Duality Theorem
5.5
we obtain:

Theorem 8.16

(Embedding Theorem)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal associated to the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then for any
E,F\in\OBAN
there are complete isometries:

\displaystyle E\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{A}(E^{\prime},F)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{A}(E,F)\qquad\textrm{ whenever }E\textrm{ is locally reflexive}

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\otimes_{\overleftarrow{\alpha}}F^{\prime}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{A}(E,F^{\prime})\qquad\textrm{ whenever }E\textrm{ and }F\textrm{ are locally reflexive.}

Remark 8.17
.

As in the comments after the Duality Theorem
5.5
and Corollary
8.12
, if
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm then the Embedding Theorem
8.16
is valid without the hypothesis of local reflexivity. In particular, this holds
in the cases
\overleftarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{\min}=\min
with
\mathfrak{A}=\CB
and
\overleftarrow{\alpha}=\overleftarrow{h}=h
with
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}_{h}
.

Given an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
, we denote its transpose o.s. tensor norm
\alpha^{t}
. That is, for every pair of operator spaces
\alpha^{t}
fulfills that

E\otimes_{\alpha}F=F\otimes_{\alpha^{t}}E

is a complete isometry with the natural identification.
As mentioned in the introduction,
\proj=\proj^{t}
,
\min=\min^{t}
but
h\neq h^{t}
. Also, it follows form the definition that
(d_{p})^{t}=g_{p}
and
(g_{p})^{t}=d_{p}.

In the classical setting the dual of a maximal ideal is also maximal, and their associated tensor norms are transposes of each other.
In the operator space setting, in order to get a similar result we once again need the additional hypothesis of local reflexivity.

Corollary 8.18
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal associated to the finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then
\mathfrak{A}^{dual}
is associated to
\alpha^{t}
.
Moreover, if
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
is the maximal mapping ideal associated to
\alpha^{t}
and
E
is a locally reflexive Banach operator space, then
\mathfrak{B}(E,F)=\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}(E,F)
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{A}\sim\alpha
and
M,N
are finite dimensional operator spaces then
\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{t}}N.
This shows that
\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}\sim\alpha^{t}.

Also, by the Representation Theorem
8.6
and Corollary
8.12
we have complete isometries

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}(E,F)

\displaystyle=(E\otimes_{(\alpha^{t})^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\cap\CB(E,F)=\big\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,\beta_{T}\in(E\otimes_{(\alpha^{t})^{\prime}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,\beta_{T^{\prime}}\in(F^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}E)^{\prime}\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}(F^{\prime},E^{\prime})\big\}=\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}(E,F)

and therefore, for
S\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{B}(E,F))
we have
\mathbf{B}_{n}(S)=\mathbf{A}_{n}(S^{\prime})
.
This is the desired conclusion.
∎

If
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is a maximal mapping ideal, it should be noted that
\mathfrak{A}^{\dual}
is not necessarily maximal as it happens in the Banach space case.
Indeed,
\mathfrak{A}=\mathcal{I}
the maximal mapping ideal of completely integral mappings, which is associated to the o.s. tensor norm
\proj
, gives us the example.
To see this, note that
\mathcal{I}^{\dual}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\proj}N
, for every
M,N\in\OFIN
. Thus, if
\mathcal{I}^{\dual}
were a maximal mapping ideal it should coincide with
\mathcal{I}
(since
\proj^{t}=\proj
).
If
W
is any non-locally reflexive o.s. then by
[
ER00
, Theorem 14.3.1]
there is
L\in\OFIN
such that the inclusion
\mathcal{N}(W,L^{*})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{I}(W,L^{*})
is not isometric.
Let
T\in\mathcal{N}(W,L^{*})
such that
\iota(T)<\nu(T)
. We have that
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(L,W^{*})=\mathcal{I}(L,W^{*})
(since
L
is finite-dimensional) and

\iota^{\dual}(T)=\iota(T^{\prime})=\nu(T^{\prime})=\nu(T)>\iota(T).

Recall that for finite-dimensional spaces
E
and
F
and linear maps
T:M\to N
,
S:N\to M
, their trace duality pairing is given by

{\langle T,S\rangle}=\tr(S\circ T).

More generally, for
T\in M_{n}(\CB(M,N))
and
S\in M_{m}(\CB(N,M))
, their trace duality (matrix) pairing is the matrix in
M_{nm}
given by

{\langle\langle T,S\rangle\rangle}=[\tr(S_{kl}\circ T_{ij})].

Definition 8.19
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal.
For
M,N\in\OFIN
,
T\in M_{n}(\CB(M,N))
and
S\in M_{m}(\CB(N,M))
, we denote by
S\circ T
the induced
mn\times mn
matrix of mappings
M\to M
.
For
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
, define

\mathbf{A}^{*}_{n}(T)=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M},S\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\,:\,\\
M\in\OFIN(E),L\in\OCOFIN(F),S\in M_{m}(\CB(F/L,M)),\mathbf{A}_{m}(S)\leq 1\big\}

and

\mathfrak{A}^{*}(E,F)=\left\{T\in\CB(E,F)\,:\,\mathbf{A}^{*}_{1}(T)<\infty\right\}.

As the following proposition shows,
(\mathfrak{A}^{*},\mathbf{A}^{*})
is a maximal mapping ideal which is associated with the so-called adjoint o.s. tensor norm.
Thus, we call
(\mathfrak{A}^{*},\mathbf{A}^{*})

the adjoint mapping ideal
of
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
.

Proposition 8.20
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a maximal mapping ideal associated to the finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
.
Then
(\mathfrak{A}^{*},\mathbf{A}^{*})
is a maximal mapping ideal, and it is associated with the adjoint o.s. tensor norm
\alpha^{*}:=(\alpha^{t})^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
be the maximal mapping ideal associated to
\alpha^{*}
. Now we show that
(\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{B})
is in fact
(\mathfrak{A}^{*},\mathbf{A}^{*})
.
First, note that, for
M,N\in\OFIN
,

\big(\mathfrak{A}(N,M)\big)^{\prime}=(N^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}M)^{\prime}=(M\otimes_{\alpha^{t}}N^{\prime})=M^{\prime}\otimes_{(\alpha^{t})^{\prime}}N=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha^{*}}N.

Hence,

\mathfrak{B}(M,N)=\big(\mathfrak{A}(N,M)\big)^{\prime}

completely isometrically, where the duality pairing is given by the canonical trace duality.
This means that for
T\in M_{n}(\CB(M,N))
we have

\mathbf{B}_{n}(T)=\sup\{\left\|{\langle\langle T,S\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{nm}}\,:\,S\in M_{m}(\mathfrak{A}(N,M)),\mathbf{A}_{m}(S)\leq 1\}.

Since
\mathfrak{B}
is maximal, it follows that for any
E,F\in\OBAN
,
and
T\in M_{n}(\CB(E,F))
,

\mathbf{B}_{n}(T)=\sup\big\{\left\|{\langle\langle(q^{F}_{L})_{n}\circ T\circ i^{E}_{M},S\rangle\rangle}\right\|_{M_{mn}}\,:\,\\
M\in\OFIN(E),L\in\OCOFIN(F),S\in M_{m}(\CB(F/L,M)),\mathbf{A}_{m}(S)\leq 1\big\}

which coincides with the definition of
\mathbf{A}^{*}_{n}(T)
.
∎

Proposition 8.21
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
The o.s. tensor norm
(g_{p^{\prime}})^{*}
is associated to the mapping ideal
\Pi_{p}
.

Proof.

Note that
(g_{p})^{*}=((g_{p})^{t})^{\prime}
is a finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm, since dual o.s. tensor norms are defined as a finite hull.

Finally, by the duality between the completely
p
-summing maps and the Chevet-Saphar o.s. tensor norms
[
CD16
]
, for any
E,F\in\OFIN

we have a canonical duality

\Pi_{p}(E,F)=(E\otimes_{d_{p^{\prime}}}F^{\prime})^{\prime}=E^{\prime}\otimes_{(d_{p^{\prime}})^{\prime}}F=E^{\prime}\otimes_{(g_{p^{\prime}})^{*}}F,

this concludes the proof.
∎

9.
Minimal o.s. mapping ideals

In the theory Banach spaces another important topic regarding the interplay between tensor products and ideals is the class of minimal operator ideals
[
DF93
, Section 22]
.
In this section we construct an operator space analogue of this concept;
an instance of this procedure already appears in the definition of nuclear mappings, as will become clear in Theorem
9.6
below.

Given a Banach mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}
, we will describe the smallest Banach mapping ideal which coincides with
\mathfrak{A}
on
\OFIN
. This mapping ideal is called the
the minimal kernel
of
\mathfrak{A}
and will be denoted by
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
. In other words, if
\mathfrak{A}
is a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
, then
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
will be the smallest Banach mapping ideal also associated to
\alpha.

In the Banach space framework, the representation theorem of minimal Banach ideals
[
DF93
, 22.2]
gives the connection with tensor products. In our setting, we will use this association to provide an o.s. structure to the mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a mapping ideal. For
E,F\in\ONORM
we define the class
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
of all mappings
T:E\to F
which admit a factorization of the form

(9.1)

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{S}

where
G,H\in\OBAN
and
R\in\mathcal{A}(E,G)
,
\widehat{T}\in\mathfrak{A}(G,H)
and
S\in\mathcal{A}(H,F)
.
For such a mapping
T
we denote

\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T):=\inf\|R\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|S\|_{\cb},

where the infimum runs over all the possible factorizations of
T
as above.

It is clear that:

Example 9.2
.

\mathcal{F}^{\min}=\mathcal{F}
.

We now see that
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
has certain structure.

Proposition 9.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal and
E,F\in\ONORM
. Then the class
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
is a vector space and
\mathbf{A}^{\min}
is a norm. Moreover, if
E,F\in\OBAN
then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F),\mathbf{A}^{\min})
is a Banach space.

Proof.

It is clear that
\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)\geq 0
, for all
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
. Also, since for each factorization of
T
as in the definition
T=S\circ\widehat{T}\circ R
we know that
\|\widehat{T}\|_{\cb}\leq\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})
and
\|T\|_{\cb}\leq\|R\|_{\cb}\|\widehat{T}\|_{\cb}\|S\|_{\cb}
we derive that

\|T\|_{\cb}\leq\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)
. Hence,
\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)=0
implies
T=0
.

Positive homogeneity is clear. Thus, to complete the first assertion we just need to check the triangle inequality. Let
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
. For a given
\varepsilon>0
there are factorizations

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{1}}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}_{1}}
\textstyle{H_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{1}}

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{2}}
\scriptstyle{T_{2}}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}_{2}}
\textstyle{H_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{2}}

with
\|R_{j}\|_{\cb}=\|S_{j}\|_{\cb}=1
and
\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T_{j}})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{j})
, for
j=1,2
. Now, consider the factorization

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}+T_{2}}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G_{1}\oplus_{\infty}G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{H_{1}\oplus_{1}H_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{S}

where
R(x)=(R_{1}(x),R_{2}(x))
,
\widehat{T}(g_{1},g_{2})=(\widehat{T_{1}}(g_{1}),\widehat{T_{2}}(g_{2}))
and
S(h_{1},h_{2})=S_{1}(h_{1})+S_{2}(h_{2})
, for every
x\in E,g_{j}\in G_{j},h_{j}\in H_{j}
,
j=1,2
.

Note that
\|R\|_{\cb}\leq\max\{\|R_{1}\|_{\cb},\|R_{2}\|_{\cb}\}=1
. By duality, the same argument yields
\|S\|_{\cb}=\|S^{\prime}\|_{\cb}\leq\max\{\|S^{\prime}_{1}\|_{\cb},\|S^{\prime}_{2}\|_{\cb}\}=1
. Writing
\widehat{T}=i_{1}\circ\widehat{T}_{1}\circ q_{1}+i_{2}\circ\widehat{T}_{2}\circ q_{2}
, where, for each
j=1,2
,
i_{j}:H_{j}\to H_{1}\oplus_{1}H_{2}
is the canonical inclusion and
q_{j}:G_{1}\oplus_{\infty}G_{2}\to G_{j}
is the canonical projection, we obtain

\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\leq\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T_{1}})+\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T_{2}})
.

In order to conclude
\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{1}+T_{2})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\left(\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{1})+\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{2})\right)
we just need to check that
R\in\mathcal{A}(E,G_{1}\oplus_{\infty}G_{2})
and
S\in\mathcal{A}(H_{1}\oplus_{1}H_{2},F)
. The first one follows easily from the fact that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are approximable. The second one is derived by duality.

Now, it remains to prove that for
E,F\in\OBAN
,
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
is complete. Let
\{T_{k}\}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
such that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{k})<\infty
. Take
(t_{k})_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}
with
1\leq t_{k}\uparrow\infty
such that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}t_{k}\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{k})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{k}).

Note that the inequality
\|\cdot\|_{\cb}\leq\mathbf{A}^{\min}
tells us that a linear mapping
T=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}T_{k}
is well defined and that the series converges in
\CB(E,F)
.

For each
k
, take a factorization

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{k}}
\scriptstyle{T_{k}}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{G_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}_{k}}
\textstyle{H_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{k}}

with
\|R_{k}\|_{\cb}=\|S_{k}\|_{\cb}=1
and
\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T}_{k})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{k})
. Now, consider

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{c_{0}(G_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{\ell_{1}(H_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{S}

where
R(x)=(\frac{R_{k}(x)}{\sqrt{t_{k}}})
,
\widehat{T}((g_{k})_{k})=((t_{k}\widehat{T}_{k}(g_{k}))_{k})
and
S((h_{k})_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{S_{k}(h_{k})}{\sqrt{t_{k}}}
, for every
x\in E,(g_{k})_{k}\in c_{0}(G_{k}),(h_{k})_{k}\in\ell_{1}(H_{k})
. It is easy to see that each mapping is well defined.

Let us see that
\widehat{T}
belongs to
\mathfrak{A}(c_{o}(G_{k}),\ell_{1}(H_{k}))
. Note that, pointwise,

(9.2)

\widehat{T}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t_{j}i_{j}\circ\widehat{T}_{j}\circ q_{j},

where, for each
j
,
i_{j}:H_{j}\to\ell_{1}(H_{k})
is the canonical inclusion and
q_{j}:c_{0}(G_{k})\to G_{j}
is the canonical projection. Since

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}(t_{j}i_{j}\circ\widehat{T}_{j}\circ q_{j})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t_{j}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T}_{j})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t_{j}\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{j})<\infty

we obtain
\widehat{T}\in\mathfrak{A}(c_{o}(G_{k}),\ell_{1}(H_{k}))
. Moreover, the series (
9.2
) converges in
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t_{j}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T}_{j})
.

Now we prove that
R\in\mathfrak{A}(E,c_{0}(G_{k}))
. Since
t_{k}\geq 1
it is clear that
\|R:E\to\ell_{\infty}(G_{k})\|_{\cb}\leq 1
. Also,
t_{k}\uparrow\infty
implies that
R
takes values in
c_{0}(G_{k})
. For a given
\varepsilon>0
let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{t_{N}}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. For each
1\leq j\leq N
, take
\widetilde{R}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}(E,G_{j})
satisfying
\|R_{j}-\widetilde{R}_{j}\|_{\cb}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2N}
. Let
\widetilde{R}:E\to c_{0}(G_{k})
be given by

x\mapsto\left(\frac{\widetilde{R}_{1}(x)}{\sqrt{t_{1}}},\dots,\frac{\widetilde{R}_{N}(x)}{\sqrt{t_{N}}},0,0,\dots\right).

Clearly,
\widetilde{R}\in\mathcal{F}(E,c_{0}(G_{k}))
and
\|R-\widetilde{R}\|_{\cb}\leq\varepsilon
. Therefore,
R\in\mathcal{A}(E,c_{0}(G_{k}))
with
\|R\|_{\cb}\leq 1
.

We finally check that
S\in\mathcal{A}(\ell_{1}(H_{k}),F)
. Note that
S^{\prime}:F^{\prime}\to\ell_{\infty}(H^{\prime}_{k})
is given by
S^{\prime}(y^{\prime})=(\frac{S_{k}^{\prime}(y^{\prime})}{\sqrt{t_{k}})_{k}})
, so the same argument as above yields
\|S\|_{\cb}=\|S^{\prime}\|_{\cb}\leq 1
. Also, analogously as before we can prove that for each
\varepsilon>0
there is
\widetilde{S}\in\mathcal{F}(\ell_{1}(H_{k}),F)
such that
\|S-\widetilde{S}\|_{\cb}\leq\varepsilon
.

From the arguments above we certainly obtained that
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
with
\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T_{k})
. This implies that
T=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}T_{k}
converges in
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
, which finishes the proof.
∎

In order to relate the tensor product
E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}F
with
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
as in the Banach space setting, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then for any
E,F\in\ONORM
the natural map

(9.3)

J:E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}F\to\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)

is a contraction of normed spaces, i.e.,

\mathbf{A}^{\min}(J(z))\leq\alpha(z;E^{\prime},F),\;\forall z\in E^{\prime}\otimes F.

Proof.

Let
z\in E^{\prime}\otimes F
,
M\in\OFIN(E^{\prime})
,
N\in\OFIN(F)
,
u\in M\otimes N
with
z=(i_{M}^{E^{\prime}}\otimes i_{N}^{F})(u)
.

From the diagram

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)}
\textstyle{i_{N}^{F}\circ T\circ q_{{}^{\circ}M}^{E}}
\textstyle{M\otimes_{\alpha}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{M}^{E^{\prime}}\otimes i_{N}^{F}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}(E/^{\circ}M,N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

we get

\mathbf{A}^{\min}(J(z))=\mathbf{A}^{\min}(i_{N}^{F}\circ T_{u}\circ q_{{}^{\circ}M}^{E})\leq\mathbf{A}(T_{u})=\alpha(u;M,N).

Since
\alpha
is finitely generated we obtain

\mathbf{A}^{\min}(J(z))\leq\alpha(z;E^{\prime},F)
.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
with factorization
T=S\circ\widehat{T}\circ R
as in (
9.1
). If
\widetilde{R}\in\mathcal{A}(E,G)
and
\widetilde{S}\in\mathcal{A}(H,F)
then

\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T-\widetilde{S}\circ\widehat{T}\circ\widetilde{R})\leq\|S-\widetilde{S}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|R-\widetilde{R}\|_{\cb}

Proof.

This is clear by the definition of the norm and the fact that

T-\widetilde{S}\circ\widehat{T}\circ\widetilde{R}=(S-\widetilde{S})\circ\widehat{T}\circ(R-\widetilde{R}).

∎

We are now ready to prove one of the main results of this section, which will allow us to provide an o.s. structure for
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
.

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then for any
E,F\in\OBAN
the natural map

J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\to\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)

is a metric surjection (at the Banach space level).

Proof.

Using Lemma
9.4
, we know that
J
is a contraction.
Take
R\in\mathcal{A}(E,G)
,
\widehat{T}\in\mathfrak{A}(G,H)
and
S\in\mathcal{A}(H,F)
.
If
z\in E^{\prime}\otimes F
satisfies
J(z)=S\circ\widehat{T}\circ R
consider the factorization

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\circ\widehat{T}\circ R}
\scriptstyle{\overline{R}}
\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{M=im(\widetilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{im(S)=N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{S}}

Thus,

(9.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha(z;E^{\prime},F)&=\alpha((\overline{R}^{\prime}\otimes i_{2})z_{T_{1}};E^{\prime},F)\leq\|\overline{R}^{\prime}\|_{\cb}\|i_{2}\|_{\cb}\alpha(z_{T_{1}};M^{\prime},N)\\
&=\|\overline{R}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(T_{1})\leq\|\overline{R}\|_{\cb}\|i_{1}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|\overline{S}\|_{\cb}\\
&=\|R\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|S\|_{\cb}.\end{split}

Now, let
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, find a factorization
T=S\circ\widehat{T}\circ R
with
\|R\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|S\|_{\cb}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)
.
Choose
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{A}(E,G)
and
(S_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{A}(H,F)
such that

\|R-R_{n}\|_{\cb}\to 0
and
\|S-S_{n}\|_{\cb}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
By Lemma
9.5
, we know that
S_{n}\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{n}
converges to
T
in
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
. Now, pick
z_{n}\in E^{\prime}\otimes F
with
J(z_{n})=S_{n}\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle J(z_{n}-z_{m})

\displaystyle=J(z_{n})-J(z_{m})

\displaystyle=S_{n}\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{n}-S_{m}\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{m}

\displaystyle=(S_{n}-S_{m})\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{n}+S_{m}\circ\widehat{T}\circ(R_{n}-R_{m})

By reasoning as in (
9.4
) and using the triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\alpha(z_{n}-z_{m};E^{\prime},F)\leq\|S_{n}-S_{m}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|R_{n}\|_{\cb}+\|S_{m}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|R_{n}-R_{m}\|_{\cb}.

Therefore,
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F
with limit, say,
z
.
since
J
is continuous,

J(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}J(z_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}S_{n}\circ\widehat{T}\circ R_{n},

where the limits are in
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F).
We have checked above that the latter limit is in fact
T
. In other words, given
T\in\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
we have found
z\in E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F
such that
J(z)=T
and, moreover, by (
9.4
) again

\displaystyle\alpha(z;E^{\prime},F)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha(z_{n};E^{\prime},F)

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{n}\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}(\widehat{T})\|R_{n}\|_{\cb}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{A}^{\min}(T)(1+\varepsilon).

This shows that the mapping
J
is a metric surjection (since we already knew that
\|J\|\leq 1)
.
∎

Using the previous theorem we can now provide an operator space structure for
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
.

Definition 9.7
.

Given a Banach mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
, we endow
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
with the unique o.s. structure such that the natural map
J
becomes a complete quotient mapping. Namely, given
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F))
, we define

\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\min}(T):=\inf\alpha_{n}(z;E^{\prime},F),

where the infimum runs all over
z\in M_{n}(E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}F)
such that
J_{n}(z)=T.

With this structure we have obviously the following:

Theorem 9.8

(Representation Theorem for Minimal Mapping Ideals)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then for any
E,F\in\OBAN
the natural operator

(9.5)

\textstyle{J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)}

is a complete quotient mapping.
In particular, if
M,N\in\OFIN
then we have a complete isometry
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}N
.

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
. Then
(\mathfrak{A}^{\min},\mathbf{A}^{\min})
is also a Banach mapping ideal associated to
\alpha
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
is the smallest Banach mapping ideal associated to
\alpha.

Proof.

To see that
(\mathfrak{A}^{\min},\mathbf{A}^{\min})
is a mapping ideal, we first see that the identity map

\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\to\CB(E,F)
is a complete contraction.

Consider the following commutative diagram:

(9.6)

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\CB(E,F),}

Now, by the Representation theorem for minimal mapping ideals we know that
J
is complete quotient. On the other hand, the inclusion
i
is a complete isometry (see Remark
5.6
) and the canonical mapping
E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\to E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F
is a complete contraction. By simply following the diagram, this implies that the descending arrow
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\dashrightarrow\CB(E,F)
is a complete contraction.

Note that condition (b’) immediately follows from the last assertion of the previous theorem:
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}N
.

Let us now focus on the ideal property. Consider
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F))
,
r\in\CB(E_{0},E)
and
s\in\CB(F,F_{0})
.
By the ideal property of
\mathcal{A}
it is easy to see that
s_{n}\circ T\circ r
lies in
M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E_{0},F_{0}))
.
To bound the norm consider the following commutative diagram:

(9.7)

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{\prime}\otimes s}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{0}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E_{0},F_{0})}
\textstyle{s\circ T\circ r.}

Since the horizontal arrows are complete quotients and the mapping

r^{\prime}\otimes s:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\to E_{0}^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F_{0}

has
\cb
-norm bounded by
\|s\|_{\cb}\|r\|_{\cb}
, it follows that

\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\min}(s_{n}\circ T\circ r)\leq\left\|s\right\|_{\cb}\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\min}(T)\left\|r\right\|_{\cb}.

By Theorem
9.8
we know that if
M,N\in\OFIN
, then there is a complete isometry
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}N
, therefore the mapping ideal
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
is associated to
\alpha
.

Finally, we show that
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
is the smallest Banach mapping ideal associated to
\alpha
. Since for any Banach mapping ideal
\mathfrak{B}
we have
\mathfrak{B}^{\min}\subset\mathfrak{B}
it is enough to see that if
\mathfrak{B}
is also associated to
\alpha
then
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}=\mathfrak{B}^{\min}
.
Indeed, this is straightforward since we have the complete quotients
J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)
and
J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{\min}(E,F)
.
∎

Now we present an operator space version of
[
DF93
, Prop. 22.1]
.

Proposition 9.10
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
be a Banach mapping ideal associated to the finitely generated o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
.
Then,

(1)

\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(M,N)=\mathfrak{A}^{\max}(M,N)=\mathfrak{A}(M,N)=M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}N
holds completely isometrically for every
M,N\in\OFIN
.

(2)

\mathfrak{A}^{\min\ \max}=\mathfrak{A}^{\max}
.

(3)

\mathfrak{A}^{\max\ \min}=\mathfrak{A}^{\min}
.

(4)

Given
E,F\in\OBAN
and
T\in M_{n}(\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F))
there are
T_{k}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{F}(E,F))
such that
\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\min}(T_{k}-T)\to 0
.

(5)

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a Banach mapping ideal. Suppose that for every
M,N\in\OFIN
we have that the inclusion
\|\mathfrak{A}(M,N)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{B}(M,N)\|_{\cb}\leq c
then

\mathfrak{A}^{\min}\subset\mathfrak{B}^{\min}
and

\|\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{\min}(E,F)\|_{\cb}\leq c
for every
E,F\in\OBAN
.

Proof.

(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
follow directly from the Representation Theorems for maximal and minimal ideals.

(4)
This can easily be derived from Lemma
9.5
.

(5)

Let
\beta
be the o.s. tensor norms
\mathfrak{B}
. The hypothesis translates into
\|M^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}N\hookrightarrow M^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\beta}N\|_{\cb}\leq c
for every
M,N\in\OFIN
. Since both norms are finitely generated, this completely bounded inclusion can be extended to
\OBAN
.
Also, by Proposition
8.9
, we know that
\mathfrak{A}^{\max}\subset\mathfrak{B}^{\max}
. Now, using
(3)
, we have
\mathfrak{A}^{\min}\subset\mathfrak{B}^{\min}
.
To conclude, consider the following diagram

(9.8)

\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\alpha}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}^{\min}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\beta}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J_{\beta}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}^{\min}(E,F).}

The result follows from the fact that
J_{\alpha}
and
J_{\beta}
are complete quotients and
\|\iota\|_{\cb}\leq c
.
∎

Definition 9.11
.

We say that a Banach mapping ideal
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})
is minimal whenever
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbf{A})=(\mathfrak{A}^{\min},\mathbf{A}^{\min})
.

We now provide some examples

(i)

\mathcal{A}=\CB^{\min}
. Moreover, given
E,F\in\OBAN
we have the completely isometric isomorphism
\mathcal{A}(E,F)=E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F
.

Indeed, it is known that the canonical mapping
i:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\hookrightarrow\CB(E,F)
is completely isometric and by the Representation Theorem,
J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F\twoheadrightarrow\CB^{\min}(E,F)
is a complete quotient. Then, the inclusion
\CB^{\min}(E,F)\hookrightarrow\CB(E,F)
has to be completely isometric. That means that the norm in
\CB^{\min}(E,F)
is
\|\cdot\|_{\cb}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is contained in any Banach mapping ideal whose norm is the cb-norm, it should be
\mathcal{A}=\CB^{\min}
.
Also,
J
is a completely isometric isomorphism and so
\mathcal{A}(E,F)=E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\min}F
.

(ii)

\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{I}^{\min}
.

Indeed, since
\mathcal{I}\sim\proj
we have a complete quotient
J:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{\proj}F\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\min}(E,F)\subset\CB(E,F)
and this coincides with the definition of
\mathcal{N}(E,F)
.

(iii)

The ideal of completely right-
p
nuclear mappings
\mathcal{N}^{p}
is minimal.

Indeed, it is clear from its definition that
\mathcal{N}^{p}=(\mathcal{N}^{p})^{\min}
because
\mathcal{N}^{p}\sim d_{p}
and
J^{p}:E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}_{d_{p}}F\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{p}(E,F)\subset\CB(E,F)
is a complete quotient.

10.
Completely projective and completely injective o.s. tensor norms

In this section we return to the abstract theory of o.s. tensor norms. In particular, we study o.s. tensor norms which behave well with respect to complete injections or projections.
We know that the the minimal o.s. tensor norm respects complete injections
[
ER00
, Prop. 8.1.5]
whereas the projective o.s. tensor norm respects complete projections
[
ER00
, Prop. 7.1.7]
.
A well-known property of the Haagerup o.s. tensor norm is that it respects both complete projections and injections
[
ER00
, Prop. 9.2.5]
. It should be noted that this cannot happen in the Banach space/normed space framework as a consequence of Grothendieck’s inequality
[
DF93
, 20.20]
.
Based on the classical definitions, we consider in this section two natural procedures on o.s. tensor norms: the completely injective hull and the completely projective hull.
Similar constructions were considered in
[
Ble92b
]
.

We begin by recalling from Section
2.5
the definitions of completely injective/projective o.s. tensor norms.

Definition 10.1
.

An o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
on
\ONORM
is called
completely right-injective
if for all
E,F,G\in\ONORM
and for all complete injections
i:F\hookrightarrow G
the mapping

id_{E}\otimes i:E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}G

is a complete injection
and it is called
completely right-projective
if for all
E,F,G\in\ONORM
and for all complete metric surjections
q:G\twoheadrightarrow F
, and all spaces
E
the mapping

id_{E}\otimes q:E\otimes_{\alpha}G\to E\otimes_{\alpha}F

is a complete metric surjection.
Left versions are defined analogously, and we say that
\alpha
is
completely injective
(resp.
completely projective
) when it is both completely left- and completely right-injective (resp. projective).
Analogous definitions will be used for other classes of spaces besides
\ONORM
(
\OFIN
, for example).

To provide some examples of these definitions, in
[
CD16
, Prop. 3.4]
it is shown that the Chevet-Saphar tensor norms
d_{p}
are completely right-projective and the Chevet-Saphar tensor norms
g_{p}
are completely left-projective. Also, by
[
Pis98
, Prop. 6.1]
, the norm
d_{2}
is completely left-injective and
g_{2}
is completely right-injective. It should also be mentioned that, by
[
Wie09
, Prop. 6.2]
, any
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm is completely projective.
For some conditions implying that a
\lambda
-o.s. tensor norm is completely injective, see
[
AKL19
, Prop. 1.2]
.

For
M,N\in\OFIN
, the complete isometry

M\otimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}N=(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\alpha}N^{\prime})^{\prime}

implies the following:

Remark 10.2
.

\alpha
is completely right-projective on
\OFIN
if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}
is completely right-injective on
\OFIN
.

In the Banach space world, the corresponding duality can be extended beyond the finite-dimensional case. Surprisingly, this property cannot be extended into
\OBAN
for general o.s. tensor norms as mentioned after Remark
12.5
(c). This is an important difference between the classical and the non-commutative theory of tensor norms. Namely, in
\OBAN
, the fact that an o.s. tensor norm is completely right-injective does not imply that its dual norm is completely right-projective.
Nevertheless, the converse is valid (see Corollary
10.4
below).

Proposition 10.3
.

If
\alpha
is finitely-generated and completely right-injective on
\OFIN
then
\alpha
is completely right-injective on
\ONORM
.

Proof.

Let
i:F\hookrightarrow G
be a complete injection and
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
.
Since
\alpha
is finitely-generated, given
\varepsilon>0
, consider
M\in\OFIN(E)
and
N\in\OFIN(G)
such that

\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);M,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);E,G).

Given
\widetilde{M}\in\OFIN(E)
and
\widetilde{N}\in\OFIN(F)
such that
z\in M_{n}(\widetilde{M}\otimes\widetilde{N})
we have:

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}(z;E,F)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{n}(z;M+\widetilde{M},\widetilde{N})=\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);M+\widetilde{M},N+i(\widetilde{N}))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);M,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);E,G),

where equalities are due to the fact that
\alpha
is completely right-injective on
\OFIN
. This concludes the proof since we always have
\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);E,G)\leq\alpha_{n}(z;E,F)
.
∎

As a consequence of Remark
10.2
, Proposition
10.3
and the fact that
\alpha^{\prime}
is finitely-generated we have:

Corollary 10.4
.

If
\alpha
is completely right-projective on
\OFIN
then
\alpha^{\prime}
is completely right-injective on
\ONORM
.

As mentioned above, the reciprocal of the previous statement does not hold in general. Nevertheless, under an additional hypothesis on the tensor norm we can actually get the converse (see Proposition
11.1
).

10.1.
Completely injective hulls

As in the Banach space setting we now describe an analogous theory for the injective hulls of given o.s. tensor norm.

Theorem 10.5
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm on
\ONORM
. Then there is a unique completely right-injective o.s. tensor norm
\alpha\backslash
on
\ONORM
such that
\beta\leq\alpha\backslash
for all completely right-injective o.s. tensor norms
\beta\leq\alpha
.
For all normed operator spaces
E
and
F\subseteq B(H)
,

E\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H)

is a complete isometry.

Proof.

Suppose that we have two completely isometric embeddings
i_{j}:F\to B(H_{j})
,
j=1,2
and define operator space structures
\alpha_{j}
on
E\otimes F
induced by

id_{E}\otimes i_{j}:E\otimes F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H_{j}).

By the injectivity of
B(H_{j})
, there exist completely bounded contractions
R_{1}:B(H_{1})\to B(H_{2})
and
R_{2}:B(H_{2})\to B(H_{1})
such that
i_{1}=R_{2}\circ i_{2}
and
i_{2}=R_{1}\circ i_{1}
.
The map
(id_{E}\otimes R_{1})\circ(id_{E}\otimes i_{1})
shows that the identity
E\otimes_{\alpha_{1}}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{2}}F
is completely contractive, and analogously so is
E\otimes_{\alpha_{2}}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{1}}F
.
Therefore, there is no ambiguity if we define
\alpha\backslash
on
E\otimes F
to be the operator space structure on
E\otimes F
induced by the particular embedding

E\otimes F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H).

From the injectivity of
\min
, the fact that
\min\leq\alpha
and the diagram

\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\min}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\min}B(H)}

it follows that
\min\leq\alpha\backslash
. Similarly, from
\alpha\leq\proj
and

\textstyle{E\otimes_{\proj}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\proj}B(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H)}

it follows that
\alpha\backslash\leq\proj
.
Thus, it is clear that
\alpha\backslash
is a reasonable operator space cross-norm.

It is not hard to see that
\alpha\backslash
has the complete metric mapping property.
Let
S\in\CB(E_{1},E_{2})
and
T\in\CB(F_{1},F_{2})
and consider completely isometric embeddings
F_{j}\subseteq B(K_{j})
.
By the injectivity of
B(H)
, there exists a completely bounded map
\widehat{T}:B(K_{1})\to B(K_{2})
such that
\|{\widehat{T}}\|_{\cb}=\left\|T\right\|_{\cb}
and

\textstyle{B(K_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{B(K_{2})}
\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes.
After tensorizing,

\textstyle{E_{1}\otimes_{\alpha}B(K_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\otimes\widehat{T}}
\textstyle{E_{2}\otimes_{\alpha}B(K_{2})}
\textstyle{E_{1}\otimes_{\alpha}F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\otimes T}
\textstyle{E_{2}\otimes_{\alpha}F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

from where we clearly see that, by the complete metric mapping property of
\alpha
,

\left\|S\otimes T:E_{1}\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F_{1}\to E_{2}\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F_{2}\right\|_{\cb}\leq\left\|S\right\|_{\cb}\left\|T\right\|_{\cb}.

Suppose that
i:F\to G
and
i_{G}:G\to B(H)
are complete injections. Since we had already shown that
\alpha\backslash
does not depend on the particular embedding into a
B(H)
and
i_{G}\circ i:F\to B(H)
is a complete injection we have, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
,

\displaystyle\alpha\backslash_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);E,G)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\backslash_{n}(z;E,F)=\alpha_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i_{G}\circ i)_{n}(z);E,B(H))

\displaystyle=\alpha\backslash_{n}((id_{E}\otimes i)_{n}(z);E,G),

showing that
\alpha\backslash
is completely-right injective.

Finally, if
\beta
is a completely right-injective o.s. tensor norm dominated by
\alpha
then the diagram

\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha\backslash}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\alpha}B(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\beta}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\otimes_{\beta}B(H)}

shows
\beta\leq\alpha\backslash
.
∎

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
\alpha
be a finitely-generated o.s. tensor norm then
\alpha\backslash
is also finitely-generated.

Proof.

Let
E
and
F
in
\ONORM
and
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
.
By the definition of the o.s. tensor norm
\alpha\backslash
if

F\subseteq B(H)
we have

(\alpha\backslash)_{n}(z;E,F)=\alpha_{n}(z;E,B(H)).

Since
\alpha
is finitely-generated, given
\varepsilon>0
there are
\widetilde{M}\in\OFIN(E)
and
\widetilde{N}\in\OFIN(B(H))
such that

\alpha_{n}(z;\widetilde{M},\widetilde{N})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}(z;E,B(H)).

Now, given
M\in\OFIN(E)
and
N\in\OFIN(F)
such that
z\in M\otimes N
we have that

\displaystyle(\alpha\backslash)_{n}(z;(M+\widetilde{M}),N)

\displaystyle=(\alpha\backslash)_{n}(z;(M+\widetilde{M}),(N+\widetilde{N}))

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{n}(z;(M+\widetilde{M}),(N+\widetilde{N}))\leq\alpha_{n}(z;\widetilde{M},\widetilde{N})

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}(z;E,B(H))=(1+\varepsilon)(\alpha\backslash)_{n}(z;E,F).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 10.7
.

The o.s. tensor norm
\alpha\backslash
given in Theorem
10.5
is called the
completely right-injective hull
of
\alpha
.
One can define analogously the
completely left-injective hull
of
\alpha
, denoted
/\penalty\alpha
, and the
completely injective hull

/\penalty\alpha\backslash:=(/\penalty\alpha)\backslash=/\penalty(\alpha\backslash).

Note that there is no ambiguity in the previous equality since
(/\penalty\alpha)\backslash=/\penalty(\alpha\backslash)
. Indeed, let
E,F\in\ONORM
and suppose that
E\subset B(H_{E})
and
F\subset B(H_{F})
; by Theorem
10.5
and its left version, the o.s. tensor norms
(/\penalty\alpha)\backslash
and
/\penalty(\alpha\backslash)
can be computed on
E\otimes F
through the complete isometry

E\otimes F\hookrightarrow B(H_{E})\otimes_{\alpha}B(H_{F}).

10.2.
Completely projective hulls

Now it is time to describe the completely projective hulls of a given o.s. tensor norm.

To do this we need some preliminary results that will be useful later. Let us start by proving an operator space version of the perturbation step in the proof of
[
DF93
, Lemma 20.2.(2)]
without appealing to local reflexivity.
The proof is inspired by
[
Pis03
, Lemma 2.13.2]
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
F\in\ONORM
, and let
\widetilde{F}
be its completion.
Given
N\in\OFIN(\widetilde{F})
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a linear map
R:N\to F
with
\left\|R\right\|_{\cb}\leq 1+\varepsilon
and
Ry=y
for all
y\in F\cap N
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{j},x_{j}^{*})_{j=1}^{m}
be a biorthogonal system for the space
N
, that is, a basis
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}
of
N
and functionals
x^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,x^{\prime}_{m}\in N^{\prime}
satisfying
x^{\prime}_{k}(x_{j})=\delta_{kj}
(we don’t assume anything about their norms).
Without loss of generality, we may suppose that
\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m_{0}}
is a basis for
F\cap N
(with
m_{0}=0
if
F\cap N=\{0\}
).
For
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
let
y_{j}=x_{j}
, and for
m_{0}+1\leq j\leq m
choose
y_{j}\in F
close enough to
x_{j}
so that

\sum_{j=m_{0}+1}^{m}\left\|x^{\prime}_{j}\right\|\left\|x_{j}-y_{j}\right\|<\varepsilon.

Define
R:N\to F
as
y\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}x^{\prime}_{j}(y)y_{j}
.
Since the identity map
I:N\to\widetilde{F}
can be written as
y\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}x^{\prime}_{j}(y)x_{j}
, it is clear that
Ry=y
for all
y\in F\cap N
, and

\left\|R\right\|_{\cb}\leq\left\|I\right\|_{\cb}+\sum_{j=m_{0}+1}^{m}\left\|x^{\prime}_{j}\right\|\left\|x_{j}-y_{j}\right\|<1+\varepsilon.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Let us quote an important lemma regarding metric surjections from the classical theory
[
DF93
, Quotient Lemma 7.4]
. This can be translated to the operator space setting canonically adapting the proof. Its statement is the following:

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
E,F\in\ONORM
,
E_{0}\subset E
a dense subspace,
q\in\CB(E,F)
a surjective mapping and consider
q_{0}=q|_{E_{0}}:E_{0}\to q(E_{0})
its restriction. Then,
q_{0}
is a complete metric surjection if and only if
\overline{\ker q_{0}}=\ker q
and
q
is a complete metric surjection.

Another ingredient needed for the construction of the completely projective hull is the following concept of right-finite hull of an o.s. tensor norm:

Definition 10.10
.

Given normed operator spaces
E
and
F
, an o.s. tensor norm
\alpha
on
\OFIN
and
u\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
, the
right-finite hull
of
\alpha
is given by

\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(u;E,F)=\inf\left\{\alpha_{n}(u;E,F_{0})\colon F_{0}\in\OFIN(F),u\in M_{n}(E\otimes F_{0})\right\}.

It is clear that
\alpha^{\rightarrow}
is a o.s. tensor norm and
\alpha\leq\alpha^{\rightarrow}\leq\overrightarrow{\alpha}
.

We are now ready to present the operator space version of
[
DF93
, Lemma 20.2]
:

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm on
\ONORM
.

(a)

If
\alpha
is completely right-projective on
\ONORM\times\OBAN
then
\alpha=\alpha^{\rightarrow}
on
\ONORM\times\OBAN
.

(b)

If
\alpha=\alpha^{\rightarrow}
on
\ONORM\times\OBAN
then
\alpha=\alpha^{\rightarrow}
on
\ONORM\times\ONORM
and for all
E,F\in\ONORM
with
\widetilde{F}
the completion of
F
, the following canonical embedding is a complete isometry:

E\otimes_{\alpha}F\hookrightarrow E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{F}.

(c)

If
\alpha
is completely right-projective on
\ONORM\times\OBAN
then it is completely right-projective on
\ONORM\times\ONORM
.

Proof.

(a)
For
G\in\OBAN
, by the construction mentioned in Section
2.5
there is a complete metric surjection
q_{G}:Z_{G}\twoheadrightarrow G
. Since
Z_{G}
is an
\ell_{1}
sum of finite dimensional spaces it is clear that it has the completely metric approximation property. By the Approximation Lemma
4.1
, for every normed operator space
E
we have that
\alpha=\alpha^{\rightarrow}
on
E\otimes Z_{G}
.

Hence, for
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes G)
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
N\in\OFIN(Z_{G})
and
u\in E\otimes N
such that
Id_{E}\otimes q_{G}(u)=z
and

\alpha_{n}(u;E,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}(z;E,G).

Therefore,

\alpha_{n}(z;E,G)\leq\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,G)\leq\alpha_{n}(z;E,q_{G}(N))\leq\alpha_{n}(u;E,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}(z;E,G).

(b)
Let
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes F)
. By the metric mapping property,

\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,\widetilde{F})\leq\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,F).

For
\varepsilon>0
let
N\in\OFIN(\widetilde{F})
with
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes N)
such that
\alpha_{n}(z;E,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,\widetilde{F})
. By Lemma
10.8
there is a completely bounded linear map
R:N\to F
with
\|R\|_{\cb}\leq 1+\varepsilon
and
Ry=y
for all
y\in F\cap N
. Note that
z\in M_{n}(E\otimes(F\cap N))
because each entry of the matrix can be seen as an operator from
E^{\prime}
to
F
and also from
E^{\prime}
to
N
. Thus, we have
(Id_{E}\otimes R)_{n}(z)=z\in M_{n}(E\otimes R(N))
.
Consequently,

\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,F)\leq\alpha_{n}((Id_{E}\otimes R)_{n}(z);E,R(N))\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{n}(z;E,N)\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}\alpha^{\rightarrow}_{n}(z;E,\widetilde{F}).

(c)
Let
E,F,G\in\ONORM
and
q:F\twoheadrightarrow G
a complete metric surjection. By Lemma
10.9
the completion
\widetilde{q}:\widetilde{F}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{G}
is also a complete metric surjection with
\ker\widetilde{q}=\overline{\ker q}
. We know from
(a)
and
(b)
that the following embeddings are complete isometries:

E\otimes_{\alpha}F\hookrightarrow E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{F}\quad\textrm{ and }\quad E\otimes_{\alpha}G\hookrightarrow E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{G},

so,
Id_{E}\otimes\widetilde{q}(E\otimes_{\alpha}F)=E\otimes_{\alpha}G
.
Now, our assumption is that
Id_{E}\otimes\widetilde{q}:E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{F}\to E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{G}
is a complete metric surjection. Thus, applying again Lemma
10.9
, in order to obtain that
Id_{E}\otimes q:E\otimes_{\alpha}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha}G
is a complete metric surjection we just need to check that
\ker(Id_{E}\otimes\widetilde{q})\subset\overline{\ker(Id_{E}\otimes q)}
(where the closure is considered inside
E\otimes_{\alpha}\widetilde{F}
). Indeed, this is true because

\ker(Id_{E}\otimes\widetilde{q})=E\otimes\ker\widetilde{q}=E\otimes\overline{\ker q}\subset\overline{E\otimes\ker q}=\overline{\ker(Id_{E}\otimes q)}.

∎

Remark 10.12
.

Observe that, using the previous lemma, if
\alpha
is completely right-projective on
\ONORM
then it is finitely-generated from the right, i.e.,
\alpha=\alpha^{\rightarrow}
.

Theorem 10.13
.

Let
\alpha
be an o.s. tensor norm on
\ONORM
. Then there is a unique completely right-projective o.s. tensor norm
\alpha/\penalty
on
\ONORM
such that
\beta\geq\alpha/\penalty
for all completely right-projective o.s. tensor norms
\beta\geq\alpha
.

For
E\in\ONORM
and
F\in\OBAN
, if
Z_{0}
is a completely projective Banach operator space and
q:Z_{0}\to F
is a complete metric surjection, then

E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{0}\to E\otimes_{\alpha/\penalty}F

is a complete metric surjection.

We recall that, given a complete operator space
E
, the completely projective space
Z_{E}
introduced in Section
2.5
is locally reflexive (as mentioned at the end of Section
2.9.1
).
With this property at hand we can now prove Theorem
10.13
.

Proof of Theorem
10.13
.

Let
F\in\OBAN
. Suppose that we have two complete quotients
q_{j}:Z_{j}\twoheadrightarrow F
,
j=1,2
where the spaces
Z_{j}
are completely projective operator spaces, and suppose that we have operator space structures
\alpha_{j}
on
E\otimes F
making

id_{E}\otimes q_{j}:E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{j}\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{j}}F

into complete quotients.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
. By the projectivity of
Z_{i}
, there exist operators
L_{1}:Z_{1}\to Z_{2}
and
L_{2}:Z_{2}\to Z_{1}
such that
\left\|L_{i}\right\|_{\cb}\leq 1+\varepsilon
satisfying
q_{2}\circ L_{1}=q_{1}
and
q_{1}\circ L_{2}=q_{2}
.
Observe that by the metric mapping property of
\alpha
,

id_{E}\otimes L_{1}:E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{1}\to E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{2}
and

id_{E}\otimes L_{2}:E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{2}\to E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{1}
have
\cb
-norm at most
1+\varepsilon
.
Since
id_{E}\otimes q_{j}:E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{j}\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{j}}F
is a complete quotient, it follows from
[
Pis03
, Prop. 2.4.1]
that the identity mappings
E\otimes_{\alpha_{1}}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{2}}F
and
E\otimes_{\alpha_{2}}F\to E\otimes_{\alpha_{1}}F
have
\cb
-norm at most
1+\varepsilon
. Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude that

E\otimes_{\alpha_{1}}F
and
E\otimes_{\alpha_{2}}F
are canonically completely isometric.
Therefore, there is no ambiguity if we define
\alpha/\penalty
on
E\otimes F
to be the operator space structure on
E\otimes F
induced by the particular quotient

E\otimes_{\alpha}Z_{F}\twoheadrightarrow E\otimes_{\alpha/}F

where
Z_{F}
is the projective space introduced in Section
2.5
.

From the projectivity of
\proj
, the fact that
\alpha\leq\proj
and the diagram

\textstyle{E\otimes_{\proj}Z_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
